\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

It is enough to show that the arrow
A(N^{\prime})_{T}\to X_{T}
is
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant.
Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(L)_{T}\times_{T}D(M)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(L)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(L^{\prime})_{T}\times_{T}D(M)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(N)_{T}\times_{T}D(M)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(L^{\prime})_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(N)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(N^{\prime})_{T}\times_{T}D(M)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(N^{\prime})_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{T}\times_{T}D(M)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{T}}

obtained by fiber product with
D(M)_{T}
. We have

A(E)_{T}\times_{T}D(M)_{T}=(A(E)\times_{{\operatorname{Spec}}({\mathbb{Z}})}T)\times_{T}D(M)_{T}=A(E)_{D(M)_{T}}

for
E\in\{N,N^{\prime},L,L^{\prime}\}
, so the left diamond is a push-out by Proposition
2.19
. Now we want to show that the lower rectangle is commutative. Consider the upper right composition in this rectangle and precompose it with the right part of the left diamond, denote this arrow by
a_{1}
. Consider the lower left composition in the rectangle and precompose it with the left part of the left diamond, denote this arrow by
a_{2}
. Now using the commutative diagrams coming from the
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant morphisms on the right, we see that
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are both equal to the composition

A(L)_{T}\times_{T}D(M)_{T}\to A(L)_{T}\to A(N^{\prime})_{T}\to X_{T}.

Using the left push-out diamond, this now implies that the lower rectangle is commutative. So the arrow
A(N^{\prime})_{T}\to X_{T}
is
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant. This finishes the proof.

2.

Let
x\in X^{N^{\prime}}(R)
.
Then
x
is a morphism
A\to R[N^{\prime}]
. Now we have an equality of compositions

(A\to R[N^{\prime}]\to R[L^{\prime}]\to R[L])=(A\to R[N^{\prime}]\to R[N^{\prime}\cap L]\to R[L]).

This shows that the diagram is commutative.
Now let us prove that it is cartesian. Let
Y={\operatorname{Spec}}(B)
be an affine
R
-scheme and let
f:Y\to X^{L^{\prime}}
and
g:Y\to X^{N}
be two morphisms such that
\mathfrak{p}_{L,L^{\prime}}\circ f=\iota_{L,N}\circ g
.
So
f
is a morphism of graded algebras
A\to B[L^{\prime}]
and
g
is a morphism of graded algebras
A\to B[N]
.
Let
m\in L^{\prime}=L+N^{\prime}
and let
A_{m}
be the
m
-graded part of
A
.
Let
x\in A_{m}
and let
\lambda_{m}
such that
f(x)=\lambda_{m}X^{m}
. Then since
\mathfrak{p}_{L,L^{\prime}}\circ f=\iota_{L,N}\circ g
, we obtain that
\lambda_{m}=0
for all
l\in L\setminus(N^{\prime}\cap L)
.
So we get
f(x)=0
for all
x\in A_{m}
for all
m\in L^{\prime}\setminus N^{\prime}
(we use that
A_{l}A_{n^{\prime}}=A_{l+n^{\prime}}
for all
l\in L\setminus(N^{\prime}\cap L)
and
n^{\prime}\in N^{\prime}
). So we obtain a unique morphism
h
from
Y
to
X^{N^{\prime}}
with the cartesian property.

∎

Proposition 3.35

Assume that
X
is separated and locally of finite presentation over
S
. Let
N
be a magnet of
M
. Write
N={\operatorname{colim}}_{i\in I}N_{i}
as a directed colimit of submonoids of
N
. Let
T\to S
be a scheme, then

X^{N}(T)={\operatorname{colim}}_{i\in I}X^{N_{i}}(T).

Proof.

For any scheme
T
, we have
A(N)_{T}=\lim_{i\in I}A(N_{i})_{T}
by
[
StP
,
Tag 01YW
]
. So
X(A(N)_{T})={\operatorname{colim}}X(A(N_{i})_{T})
by
[
StP
,
Tag 049J
]
. Let
A(N)_{T}\to X_{T}
be an
A(M)_{T}
-equivariant morphism. By the previous assertion this factorizes through a morphism
A(N_{i})_{T}\xrightarrow{f}X_{T}
. We want to show that
f
is
A(M)_{T}
-equivariant. Consider the diagram

\textstyle{A(M)_{T}\times A(N_{i})_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{T}}
\textstyle{A(M)_{T}\times A(N)_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the horizontal arrows
(f_{1},f_{2})
correspond to
(g,x)\mapsto f(gx)
and
(g,x)\mapsto gf(x)
. Since
X
is separated, the kernel of
(f_{1},f_{2})
is a closed subscheme of
A(M)_{T}\times A(N_{i})_{T}
(the proof of
[
GW
, Def./Prop. 9.7]
works in this context). Since the schematic image of the vertical morphism
\phi
is
A(M)_{T}\times_{T}A(N_{i})_{T}
and because
f_{1}\circ\phi=f_{2}\circ\phi
, we have an equality of schemes
\ker(f_{1},f_{2})=A(M)_{T}\times_{T}A(N_{i})_{T}
. So we have an equality of morphisms of schemes
f_{1}=f_{2}
, and so
f
is equivariant.
We deduce that

X^{N}(T)={\operatorname{colim}}_{i\in I}X^{N_{i}}(T).

∎

4
Attractors with prescribed limits

We introduce in this section another functor.
Let
X
be an algebraic space over a base scheme
S
. Let
M
be a commutative monoid and let
A(M)_{S}
be the associated diagonalizable monoid scheme over
S
.
Assume that
A(M)_{S}
acts on
X
. Let
N\subset M
be a magnet. Let
F
be a face of
N
. Let
Z
be an other
S
-functor with a monomorphism
Z\to X^{F}
.
We now introduce the attractor
X_{F,Z}^{N}
associated to the magnet
N
under the action of
A(M)_{S}
on
X
with prescribed limit in
Z
relatively to the face
F
. Recall that we have a canonical morphism
X^{N}\to X^{F}
(cf. Fact
3.15
). Since
Z(T)\subset X^{F}(T)
for any
S
-scheme
T
, the following definition makes sense.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
X_{F,Z}^{N}
be the contravariant functor

(Sch/S)\to Set,(T\to S)\mapsto\{f\in X^{N}(T)\mid\text{ the image of }f\text{ in }X^{F}(T)\text{ belongs to }Z(T)\}.

If
F=N^{*}
, we omit
F
in the notation, i.e. we put
X^{N}_{N^{*},Z}=:X^{N}_{Z}
.

Proposition 4.2

We have a canonical isomorphism
X^{N}_{F,Z}\simeq X^{N}\times_{X^{F}}Z
.

Proof.

Clear since

\displaystyle X_{F,Z}^{N}(T)

\displaystyle=\{f\in X^{N}(T)\mid\text{ the image of }f\text{ in }X^{F}(T)\text{ belongs in }Z(T)\}

\displaystyle=\{(f,g)\in X^{N}(T)\times Z(T)\mid~f=g\text{ in }X^{F}(T)\}

\displaystyle=X^{N}(T)\times_{X^{F}(T)}Z(T).

∎

Fact 4.3

Assume that
X
is separated. Then we have a canonical monomorphism
X^{N}_{F,Z}\to X^{N}
of
S
-functors. In particular we have a canonical monomorphism
X^{N}_{F,Z}\to X
of
S
-functors.

Proof.

Clear by Corollary
3.29
.
∎

5
Base change of affine étale morphisms along faces

Let
S
be a scheme and let
X
be an

S
-algebraic space with an action of
D(M)_{S}
for some finitely generated abelian group
M
. Let
N
be a magnet of
M
.

Proposition 5.1

Let
Q
be a face of
N
and let
X^{N}\to X^{Q}
be the associated morphism on attractors. Let
U
be an
S
-affine scheme.
Let
U\to X
be an étale,
D(M)_{S}
-equivariant morphism of
algebraic spaces. Assume that one of the following conditions hold

1.

N
is finitely generated as monoid,

2.

X
is separated,

then the natural map
U^{N}\to X^{N}\times_{X^{Q}}U^{Q}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We need some terminologies on monoids. Recall that a monoid is fine if it is cancellative and finitely generated. Recall also that a monoid
L
is sharp if
L^{*}=0
. See
[
Og
, §I]
for more details on monoids.

1.

Firstly, we remark that it is enough to treat the case
Q=N^{*}.
Indeed assume that Proposition
5.1
is true for the face of invertible elements. Since
Q^{*}=N^{*}
, we have

X^{N}\times_{X^{Q}}U^{Q}=X^{N}\times_{X^{Q}}(X^{Q}\times_{X^{N^{*}}}U^{N^{*}})=X^{N}\times_{X^{N^{*}}}U^{N^{*}}=U^{N}.

So we assume
Q=N^{*}
. We now remark that we can assume
N^{*}=0
and that
N
is fine and sharp using the map
M\to M/N^{*}
and Proposition
3.30
. So we now assume that
Q=0
and
N
is fine and sharp and we adapt
[
Ri16
, Lemma 1.11]
.
It is enough to construct the inverse morphism

X^{N}\times_{X^{0}}U^{0}\to U^{N}
.
For this let
p:T\to S
be an
S
-scheme and let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T=A(0)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(N)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

be a diagram corresponding to a
T
-point of
X^{N}\times_{X^{0}}U^{0}
. We
want to find a diagonal filling
A(N)_{T}\to U_{T}
. Let
I
be the kernel of
{\mathcal{O}}_{T}[N]\to{\mathcal{O}}_{T}[0]
, i.e the ideal associated to
N\setminus 0
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{T}[N]
. Let
V(I^{k})={\operatorname{Spec}}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}_{T}[N]/I^{k})
be the
infinitesimal neighbourhoods of
A(0)_{T}
inside
A(N)_{T}
. Since
U_{T}\to X_{T}

is étale, it is smooth by
[
StP
,
Tag 04XX
]
, and formally smooth by
[
StP
,
Tag 02H6
]
, so by the infinitesimal lifting property the morphism

f_{0}:T\to U_{T}
lifts uniquely
to a compatible family of morphisms
f_{k}:V(I^{k})\to U_{T}
. These liftings
are equivariant because the two maps
D(M)_{T}\times V(I^{k})\to U_{T}
,

(g,x)\mapsto f_{k}(gx)
and
(g,x)\mapsto gf_{k}(x)
are common liftings
of the map
D(M)_{T}\times A(0)_{T}\to U_{T}
,
(g,x)\mapsto f_{0}(gx)=gf_{0}(x)

hence by uniqueness they are equal.
Writing
U={\operatorname{Spec}}_{S}({\mathcal{A}})
for a
M
-graded quasi-coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
, we have and
U\times_{S}T={\operatorname{Spec}}_{T}({\mathcal{A}}_{T})
where
{\mathcal{A}}_{T}=p^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
.
Moreover we have a family of
M
-graded morphisms

{\mathcal{A}}_{T}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{T}[N]/I^{k}
. This means that
{({\mathcal{A}}_{T})}_{m}
goes to
0
when

m\not\in N
and to
({\mathcal{O}}_{T}[N]/I^{k})_{m}
when
m\in N
.
The induced morphism to the completion

{\mathcal{A}}_{T}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{T}[[N]]
has image in
{\mathcal{O}}_{T}[N]
, yielding the desired lifting
A(N)_{T}\to U_{T}
(cf.
[
Og
, Chap. I,§3 Prop. 3.6.1]
for the local description of the completion
{\mathcal{O}}_{T}[[N]]
).

2.

Let
T\to S
be a scheme. Write
N={\operatorname{colim}}_{i\in I}N_{i}
as a colimit of finitely generated monoids. Using (i) and Proposition
3.35
we have

\displaystyle U^{N}(T)

\displaystyle={\operatorname{colim}}_{i\in I}U^{N_{i}}(T)

\displaystyle={\operatorname{colim}}_{i\in I}\big(X^{N_{i}}(T)\times_{X^{Q}(T)}U^{Q}(T)\big)

\displaystyle={\operatorname{colim}}_{i\in I}\big(X^{N_{i}}(T)\big)\times_{X^{Q}(T)}U^{Q}(T)

\displaystyle=X^{N}(T)\times_{X^{Q}(T)}U^{Q}(T)

\displaystyle=(X^{N}\times_{X^{Q}}U^{Q})(T).

∎

6
Fixed-point-reflecting atlases

Let
M
be an abelian group. Let
S
be a scheme. Let
X
be a quasi-separated
S
-space endowed with an action of
D(M)_{S}
.

We discuss fixed-point-reflecting atlases in this section. Roughly, fixed-point-reflecting étale atlases are atlases such that we can learn from the charts the fixed points of our space. This concept makes sense for an action of an arbitrary group scheme and is well-studied. In this section we introduce several related concepts and definitions,
Z
-FPR morphisms and strongly-FPR morphisms, useful and motivated by algebraic magnetism (
Z
is a subgroup of
M
here). A strongly-FPR atlas is
Z
-FPR for all subgroups
Z\subset M
. We formulate a theorem (Theorem
6.8
) that provides the existence of
Z
-FPR atlases and a conjecture (Conjecture
6.9
) about the existence of strongly-FPR atlases. In this work,
Z
-FPR atlases are used to prove representability results (Proposition
8.1
and Theorem
8.3
) and strongly-FPR atlases are needed to study magnets (Theorem
15.4
).
In the next section (Section
7
), we will notice that Conjecture
6.9
is true in many cases (namely for Zariski locally linearizable actions, e.g. Sumihiro’s actions
7.4
) in particular one has
Z
-FPR atlases in this case. Romagny’s appendix offers a proof of Theorem
6.8
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
f:U\to X
be a
D(M)_{S}
-equivariant morphism of
S
-spaces.

1.

We say that
f
is fixed-point reflecting (FPR) if the canonical morphism of functors
U^{D(M)_{S}}\to U\times_{X}X^{D(M)_{S}}
is an isomorphim.

2.

We say that
f
is
Z
-FPR if it is fixed-point reflecting for the induced action of
D(M/Z)_{S}
, i.e. if the canonical morphism of functors
U^{D(M/Z)_{S}}\to U\times_{X}X^{D(M/Z)_{S}}
is an isomorphim.

3.

We say that
f
is strongly-FPR if it is
Z
-FPR for all subgroups
Z\subset M
.

4.

We say that
f
is an equivariant atlas if
f
is étale and surjective and
U=\coprod_{\tau\in\mathscr{A}}U_{\tau}
is the disjoint union of
D(M)_{S}
-stable
S
-affine schemes; moreover in this case we say that the equivariant atlas
f
is
S
-affine if
\mathscr{A}
may be chosen finite.

5.

We say that
f
is a
Z
-FPR atlas if
f
is a
Z
-FPR equivariant atlas.

6.

We say that
f
is a strongly-FPR atlas if
f
is a strongly-FPR equivariant atlas.

Remark 6.2
.

Since a finite disjoint union of
S
-affine schemes is
S
-affine, if an equivariant atlas is
S
-affine then we can assume that
\mathscr{A}
is a singleton. In other words an
S
-affine equivariant atlas for
X
is a
D(M)_{S}
-equivariant étale surjective morphism
U\to S
where
U
is an
S
-affine scheme.

The following is an immediate generalization of
[
Ri16
, Lemma 1.10]
.

Proposition 6.3

Assume that
M\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{r}
for some integer
r
and let
U\to X
be an étale
D(M)_{S}
-equivariant morphism, then
U^{D(M)_{S}}\to X^{D(M)_{S}}\times_{X}U
is an isomorphism. In other words, if
M
is torsion-free, then every étale morphism is FPR.

Proof.

The proof of
[
Ri16
, Lemma 1.10]
works replacing ”
\mathbb{G}_{m}
” by ”
D({\mathbb{Z}}^{r})
”.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

In his appendix, Romagny provides a generalization of Proposition
6.3
to arbitrary flat group schemes with connected fibers and in particular provides a detailled proof of Proposition
6.3
, cf. Theorem
A.1
.

Remark 6.5
.

If
M
is not torsion-free, and
U\to X
is an étale
D(M)_{S}
-equivariant morphism, then
U^{D(M)_{S}}\to X^{D(M)_{S}}\times_{X}U
is obviously not an isomorphism in general. For example, take
M={\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
,
S={\operatorname{Spec}}({\mathbb{Z}})
,
X=S
endowed with the trivial action of
D(M)
and
U=D(M)
endowed with the non-trivial action of itself by multiplication. Then the
D(M)
-equivariant morphism
U\to S
is smooth of relative dimension zero and so it is étale; but
U^{D(M)}=\emptyset
,
X^{D(M)}=X
and therefore
U^{D(M)}\not\cong X^{D(M)}\times_{X}U
.

Remark 6.6
.

Let us provide an example of a space
X
endowed with an action of a diagonalizable group
D(M)
and an FPR equivariant atlas
U\to X
that is not strongly-FPR. Choose an exact sequence of abelian groups
0\to Z\to M\to M/Z\to 0
with
Z\neq M
and
M/Z
finite. Then
f:D(M)\to D(Z)
is étale, surjective and
D(M)
-equivariant. Put
U=D(M)
and
X=D(Z)
. Since
U^{D(M)}=X^{D(M)}=\emptyset
,
f
is FPR. Now
f
is not
Z
-FPR because
U^{D(M/Z)}=\emptyset
and
X^{D(M/Z)}=X
.

Lemma 6.7

Let
X,X^{\prime}
be
S
-algebraic spaces endowed with actions of
D(M)_{S}
. Let
Z
be a subgroup of
M
. Let
X^{\prime}\to X
be a
D(M)_{S}
-equivariant affine morphism of
S
-algebraic spaces. Let
U\to X
be a
Z
-FPR atlas of
X
. Then

1.

The canonical morphism
U\times_{X}X^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
is a
Z
-FPR atlas of
X^{\prime}
.

2.

If
U\to X
is an
S
-affine
Z
-FPR atlas of
X
, then
U\times_{X}X^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
is an
S
-affine
Z
-FPR atlas of
X^{\prime}
.

Proof.

(i) Since
X^{\prime}\to X
is an affine morphism,
U\times_{X}X^{\prime}\to U
is affine and so the composition
U\times_{X}X^{\prime}\to S
is a disjoint union of
S
-affine schemes. The morphism
U\times_{X}X^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
is étale and surjective because
U\to X
is so. By Proposition
3.7
, we have canonical identifications

(X^{\prime}\times_{X}U)^{Z}={X^{\prime}}^{Z}\times_{X^{Z}}U^{Z}={X^{\prime}}^{Z}\times_{X^{Z}}(U\times_{X}X^{Z})={X^{\prime}}^{Z}\times_{X}U={X^{\prime}}^{Z}\times_{X^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}\times_{X}U).

Assertion (ii) is now immediate.
∎

Theorem
6.8
was motivated by finding a generalization of Proposition
6.3
for general diagonalizable group schemes. Theorem
6.8
is essentially a theorem
of Alper, Hall and Rydh
[
AHR21
]
. The present version of Theorem
6.8
was formulated by Mayeux. The proof of Theorem
6.8
could be seen as a corollary of
[
AHR21
]
. Establishing Theorem
6.8
as a corollary of
[
AHR21
]
is due to Romagny and is the topic of the appendix. Note that Theorem
6.8
is numbered as Theorem
A.2
in the appendix.

Theorem 6.8

Assume that
M
is finitely generated as abelian group.
Let
X
be a quasi-separated
S
-algebraic space locally of
finite presentation endowed with an action of
D(M)_{S}
.
Let
Z\subset M
be a subgroup and assume that one of the
following assertions holds:

1.

X
is separated over
S
,

2.

M/Z
is torsion-free.

Then there exists a
Z
-FPR atlas
U\to X
, which may be chosen
quasi-compact (in particular
S
-affine here) if
X\to S
is quasi-compact.

Proof.

The proof is given in the Appendix of this article, cf. Theorem
A.2
.
∎

In fact the author expects that the following stronger assertions should be true. We will study the correct generality in which this holds. We think that this is an interesting problem.

Conjecture 6.9

Assume that
M
is finitely generated as abelian group. Let
S
be an arbitrary scheme.
Let
X
be a separated
S
-algebraic space locally of
finite presentation endowed with an action of
G=D(M)_{S}
. The following assertions hold.

1.

There exists a strongly-FPR atlas
U\to X
.

2.

If
X/S
is quasi-compact, there exists an
S
-affine strongly-FPR atlas
U\to X
.

Note that §
7
already ensures the existence of strongly-FPR atlases for Sumihiro actions.

7
Zariski locally linearizable actions

We proceed with the notation from §
6
. We recall the definition of Zariski locally linearizable actions and explain that they have strongly-FPR atlases. We recall that some actions are far from being locally linearizable; this gives a reason to use étale atlases (cf. §
6
) and the theory of algebraic spaces.

Fact 7.1

Let
U\to X
be a
D(M)_{S}
-equivariant open immersion, then for any subgroup
Z\subset M
,

U^{D(M/Z)_{S}}\to X^{D(M/Z)_{S}}\times_{X}U

is an isomorphism. In other words, every
D(M)_{S}
-equivariant open immersion is
Z
-FPR for all subgroups.

Proof.

This works for any equivariant monomorphism.
For any scheme
T
over
S
,
U(T)\times_{X(T)}X^{D(M/Z)_{S}}(T)=U^{D(M/Z)_{S}}(T)
.
∎

Definition 7.2
.

The action of
D(M)_{S}
on
X
is Zariski locally linearizable if there are
S
-affine open
D(M)_{S}
-stable subspaces of
X
covering
X
. We also refer to these actions as Sumihiro actions or Sumihiro spaces.

Fact 7.3

Let
X
be an
S
-space endowed with a Zariski locally linearizable action of
D(M)_{S}
, then there exists a strongly-FPR equivariant atlas.

Proof.

Let
U
be the disjoint union of
S
-affine open
D(M)_{S}
-stable subspaces of
X
covering
X
, then
U\to X
is as required by Fact
7.1
.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

Assume that
M
is finitely generated. The result
[
Su75
, Corollary 3.11]
shows that if
D(M)_{S}
is smooth over
S
and if
X
is a scheme and satisfies the condition (N) (e.g.
X
is normal, cf.
[
Su75
, 3.4, 3.5]
), then the action of
D(M)_{S}
on
X
is Zariski locally linearizable.

Remark 7.5
.

There exists a simple example of an action of
D({\mathbb{Z}})_{S}
on a quasi-separated
locally finitely presented scheme
X/S
that is not Zariski locally linearizable (e.g. cf.
[
Ri16
, §0.2]
).

8
Representability and properties

Let
M
be an abelian group. Let
S
be an arbitrary scheme.
Let
X
be an
S
-algebraic space endowed with an action of
D(M)_{S}
. We prove that attractors are representable once we know the existence of FPR atlases. Recall that Theorem
6.8
or Section
7
provide FPR atlases in great generality, so that our representabilty results hold in great generality.

Proposition 8.1

Let
Z\subset M
be a
subgroup. Assume that there exists a
Z
-FPR atlas for
X
under the action of
D(M)_{S}
(cf. Definition
6.1
). Then the attractor space
X^{Z}
(which identifies with the fixed space
X^{D(M/Z)_{S}}
by Proposition
3.33
) is representable by a closed subspace of
X
.

Proof.

Let
U
be a
Z
-FPR atlas. We have a cartesian square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.17845pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.17845pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{U^{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.17845pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.78947pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 34.17845pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.9326pt\raise-24.78947pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.95901pt\raise-32.12279pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.39789pt\raise-32.12279pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 37.39789pt\raise-32.12279pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

By Proposition
3.21
the left vertical arrow is a closed immersion of schemes. The lower arrow is surjective étale. So by
[
StP
,
Tag 03I2
]
,
X^{Z}
is an algebraic space and the right vertical arrow is a closed immersion. We used that being a closed immersion
is stable under base change, fppf-local on the base and closed immersions satisfy fppf-descent.
∎

Remark 8.2
.

Theorem
A.1
of the appendix provides a result similar to Proposition
8.1
about closedness.

Theorem 8.3

Let
N\subset M
be a magnet, i.e. an arbitrary submonoid. Let
N^{*}\subset N
be the face of intertible elements of
N
. Assume that there exists a
N^{*}
-FPR atlas for
X
under the action of
D(M)_{S}
(cf. Definition
6.1
). Assume that one of the following conditions hold

(a)

X
is separated over
S
, or

(b)

N
is finitely generated as monoid,

then the attractor
X^{N}
is representable by an
algebraic space over
S
. Moreover

1.

The morphism of algebraic spaces
X^{N}\to X^{N^{*}}
is affine.

2.

If
X/S
is quasi-separated, then
X^{N}/S
is quasi-separated.

3.

If
X/S
is separated, then
X^{N}/S
is separated.

4.

If
X/S
is locally of finite presentation, then
X^{N}/S
is locally of finite presentation.

5.

If
X/S
is quasi-compact then
X^{N}/S
is quasi-compact.

6.

If
X/S
if of finite presentation, then
X^{N}/S
is of finite presentation.

7.

If
X
is a scheme then
X^{N}
is a scheme.

Proof.

Let
U\to X
be a
N^{*}
-FPR atlas. Using Proposition

5.1
, we obtain a diagram with
cartesian squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{N^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{N^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}

The vertical maps are étale and surjective,
and we know from Theorem
3.20
that
U^{N}
is
representable by a disjoint sum of
S
-affine schemes. By Proposition
8.1
,
X^{N^{*}}
is an
S
-algebraic space. It follows
from
[
StP
,
Tag 03I2
]
that
X^{N}
is representable.

Now let us prove the listed properties.
We have a cartesian square

\textstyle{U^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U^{N^{*}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{N^{*}}.}

As explained before, the lower horizontal arrow is étale and surjective. The left vertical arrow is affine because
U
is
S
-affine and
U^{N}
and
U^{N^{*}}
are
S
-affine schemes by Theorem
3.20
. Now, we apply
[
StP
,
Tag 03I2
]
to conclude that the right vertical arrow is affine. We used that being affine is preserved under base change, fppf local on the base and satisfies descent for fppf coverings. Assume that
X
is locally of finite presentation, i.e. commutes with colimits of affine schemes in
({Sch}/S)_{\mathrm{fppf}}
, then the functor
X^{N}
commutes with colimits of affine schemes in
({Sch}/S)_{\mathrm{fppf}}
, i.e.
X^{N}
is locally of finite presentation.
Assume that
X
is separated (resp. quasi-separated, quasi-compact, resp. is a scheme). Then
X^{N^{*}}
is separated (resp. quasi-separated, quasi-compact, resp. is a scheme) because it is closed in
X
(cf. Proposition
8.1
). Since
X^{N}\to X^{N^{*}}
is affine, in particular
representable, quasi-compact and separated it follows that
X^{N}
is separated (resp. quasi-compact, resp. is a scheme). Note that by definition
X
is of finite presentation if it is locally of finite presentation, quasi-compact and quasi-separated.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

Note that
Proposition
3.30
(iii) and Theorem
8.3
together show representability of many attractors under actions of diagonalizable monoid schemes outside affine cases.

Proposition 8.5

Assume that
M
is finitely generated as abelian group.
Assume that
X\to S
is quasi-compact and separated. Then there exists a finitely generated submonoid
N_{c}
of
N
such that
X^{N}=X^{N_{c}}
and
N^{*}=N_{c}^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
\kappa:{\mathbb{N}}\to N
be a bijection. For any
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
N_{i}
be the submonoid of
N
generated by
\{N^{*}\cup\kappa({\mathbb{N}}_{\leqslant i})\}
. Then
N_{i}^{*}=N^{*}
and
N_{i}
is finitely generated for any
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
, moreover
\bigcup_{i\geqslant 0}N_{i}=N
. By Proposition
3.35

X^{N}(T)={\operatorname{colim}}X^{N_{i}}(T).

Let
U\to X
be an
S
-affine
N^{*}
-FPR atlas as in Theorem
6.8
. Using that
U
is an
S
-affine scheme of finite presentation over
S
, there exists an integer
c
such that
U^{N_{c}}=U^{N_{i}}
for all
i\geqslant c
. To see this, write
U
as the spectrum of a graded
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-algebra, consider degrees of a finite set of generators of this
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-algebra and use that gradings of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-algebras are preserved under equivariant morphisms of
S
-affine schemes.
Let
i\geqslant c
, and consider the diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 12.62494pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.62494pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{U^{N_{c}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.62494pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 36.62494pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{N_{c}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.82558pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-12.3353pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{U^{N_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.91458pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 36.91458pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{N_{i}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

The horizontal arrows are étale and surjective using Proposition
5.1
. Moreover, the left vertical arrow is an isomorphism since
i\geqslant c
. Furthermore, the diagram is a cartesian square because by Proposition
5.1

\displaystyle U^{N_{i}}\times_{X^{N_{i}}}X^{N_{c}}

\displaystyle\simeq(U^{N^{*}}\times_{X^{N^{*}}}X^{N_{i}})\times_{X^{N_{i}}}X^{N_{c}}

\displaystyle\simeq U^{N^{*}}\times_{X^{N^{*}}}X^{N_{c}}

\displaystyle\simeq U^{N_{c}}.

Using
[
StP
,
Tag 03I2
]
for the property ”isomorphism”, we deduce that for all
i\geqslant c
,
X^{N_{i}}=X^{N_{c}}.

This implies that
X^{N}=X^{N_{c}}
.
∎

9
Hochschild cohomology for diagonalizable monoids via formulas

Let
S
be a scheme and
B
be an algebraic space over
S
.
Let
B_{spaces,fppf}
be the small fppf site of
B
(defined as in the case of schemes, e.g. cf.
[
Sc17
]
). In this section
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
is the canonical sheaf of rings on
B_{spaces,fppf}
.

9.1
Affine morphisms and sheaves

Proposition 9.1

Let
f:X\to B
be an affine morphism of algebraic spaces. Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be the quasi-coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-algebra of
X
. Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a quasi-coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module. Then

f_{*}f^{*}{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}}.

Proof.

Let
f^{*}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
be the canonical morphism coming from adjunction. It induces a canonical morphism

f^{*}{\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}f^{*}f_{*}{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}\to f^{*}{\mathcal{F}}.

Since
{\mathcal{A}}=f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
, we get obtain a canonical morphism
f^{*}{\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}f^{*}{\mathcal{A}}\to f^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
. Now using
[
StP
,
Tag 03EL
]
, we obtain a canonical morphism

f^{*}({\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}})\to f^{*}{\mathcal{F}}.

By adjunction, we finally get a canonical morphism
{\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}}\to f_{*}f^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
. It remains to prove that it is an isomorphism. For this, we reduce to the affine case and apply
[
StP
,
Tag 01I8
]
.
∎

9.2
Fppf Hochschild monoid cohomology over algebraic spaces

Let
H
be a monoid algebraic space over
B
. Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
be the sheaf of rings of
B
on
B_{spaces,fppf}
.

Definition 9.2
.

A
H
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module over
B_{spaces,fppf}
is an
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module
{\mathcal{F}}
in the sense of
[
StP
,
Tag 03CW
]
such that
H
acts
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-linearly on
{\mathcal{F}}
, i.e:

1.

For any
T\to B\in B_{spaces,fppf}
,
H(T)
acts on
{\mathcal{F}}(T)
. Moreover for any
g\in H(T),x,y\in{\mathcal{F}}(T)
and
a\in{\mathcal{O}}_{B}(T)
, we have
g\cdot(x+ay)=g\cdot x+a(g\cdot y)
.

2.

If
T^{\prime}\to T
is a morphism in
B_{spaces,fppf}
, the action of
H(T^{\prime})
on
\mathcal{F}(T^{\prime})
is compatible with the action of
H(T)
on
{\mathcal{F}}(T)
.

Remark 9.3
.

Note that Definition
9.2
is similar to Definition
2.20
, but is different.

Definition 9.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a
H
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module over
B_{spaces,fppf}
.
Let
n\geqslant 0
be an integer. We put

C^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})=Mor_{B_{spaces,fppf}}(H^{n},{\mathcal{F}})=\text{The set of natural transformations from }H^{n}\text{ to }{\mathcal{F}},

where
H^{n}
and
{\mathcal{F}}
are seen as functors from
B_{spaces,fppf}
to
{\operatorname{Sets}}
. The set
C^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})
is canonically endowed with a structure of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}(B)
-module via

\text{ for any }\Theta,\Psi\in C^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}}),a\in{\mathcal{O}}_{B}(B),T\in B_{spaces,fppf};(\Theta+a\Psi)(T)=\Theta(T)+{a\mid_{T}}\Psi(T).

We have a morphism of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}(B)
-modules

{\partial:C^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})\to C^{n+1}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}

sending
\Theta
to
\partial\Theta
where
\partial\Theta
is the transformation from
H^{n+1}
to
{\mathcal{F}}
such that for any
T\in B_{spaces,fppf}
,
\partial\Theta(T)
is the map sending
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n+1}\in H^{n+1}(T)
to

g_{1}\cdot\Big(\Theta(T)\big(g_{2},\ldots,g_{n+1}\big)\Big)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}\Theta(T)\big(g_{1},\ldots,g_{i}g_{i+1},\ldots,g_{n+1})+(-1)^{n+1}f(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\big)\in{\mathcal{F}}(T).

We also have an internal version of
C^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})
as follows.

Definition 9.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a
H
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module.
Let
n\geqslant 0
be an integer. We put for any
V\in B_{spaces,fppf}

\underline{C^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}(V)=C^{n}(H|_{V},{\mathcal{F}}|_{V})=Mor_{V_{spaces,fppf}}(H^{n}|_{V},{\mathcal{F}}|_{V}).

The functor
\underline{C^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}
is canonically endowed with a structure of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module.
We have a morphism of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-modules
{\underline{\partial}:\underline{C^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}\to\underline{C^{n+1}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}}
induced by
\partial
.

Remark 9.6
.

For any positive integer
n
the composition
\underline{\partial}\circ\underline{\partial}
is the zero map.

Definition 9.7
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a
H
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module over
B_{spaces,fppf}
. We put

\displaystyle Z^{n}_{monoid}(H,{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle=\ker(C^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{\partial}C^{n+1}(H,{\mathcal{F}}))\text{ for any }n\geqslant 0

\displaystyle B^{0}_{monoid}(H,{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle B^{n}_{monoid}(H,\cal F)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{im}(C^{n-1}(H,{\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{\partial}C^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})\text{ for any }n\geqslant 1

\displaystyle H^{n}_{monoid}(H,{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle=\frac{Z^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}{B^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}\text{ for any }n\geqslant 0.

\displaystyle\underline{Z^{n}_{monoid}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}

\displaystyle=\ker(\underline{C^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}\xrightarrow{\underline{\partial}}\underline{C^{n+1}(H,{\mathcal{F}})})\text{ for any }n\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\underline{B^{0}_{monoid}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\underline{B^{n}_{monoid}(H,\cal F)}

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathrm{im}(C^{n-1}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}\xrightarrow{\underline{\partial}}\underline{C^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}\text{ for any }n\geqslant 1

\displaystyle\underline{H^{n}_{monoid}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{~\underline{Z^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}~}{~\underline{B^{n}(H,{\mathcal{F}})}~}\text{ for any }n\geqslant 0.

We finish this subsection with the description of
Z^{n}_{monoid}
and
B^{n}_{monoid}
for
n=1
.

Remark 9.8
.

We have

Z^{1}_{monoid}(H,{\mathcal{F}})=\\
\{c\in C^{1}(H,{\mathcal{F}})\mid\forall T\in B_{spaces,fppf},\forall g,g^{\prime}\in H(T),~c(gg^{\prime})=c(g)+g\cdot c(g^{\prime})\}.

B^{1}_{monoid}(H,{\mathcal{F}})=\{b\in C^{1}(H,{\mathcal{F}})\mid\exists v\in{\mathcal{F}}(B);\forall T\in B_{sp.,fppf},\forall g\in G(T),b(g)=g\cdot v_{T}-v_{T}\}.

9.3
Modules under affine monoid algebraic spaces

Let
M
be a finitely generated cancellative monoid. The structural morphism
A(M)_{B}\to B
and the multiplication morphism
A(M)_{B}\times_{B}A(M)_{B}\to A(M)_{B}
are surjective and finitely presented by Facts
2.6
and
2.10
. The morphism
A(M)_{B}\to B
is flat. We assume in this section that the multiplication morphism
A(M)_{B}\times_{B}A(M)_{B}\to A(M)_{B}
is flat. We refer to Proposition
2.8
for a criterion. For example if
M
is a group, the multiplication morphism
D(M)_{B}\times_{B}D(M)_{B}\to D(M)_{B}
is flat.

Proposition 9.9

Let
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}
be two
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-modules (over
B_{spaces,fppf}
). Assume
{\mathcal{G}}
is quasi-coherent. Let

X^{\prime}={\operatorname{Spec}}_{B}({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{m}X={\operatorname{Spec}}_{B}({\mathcal{A}})\xrightarrow{f}B.

be affine morphisms of
S
-spaces. Assume
m
and
f
are flat and finitely presented. Then we have a canonical identification

\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})(X)={\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}}).

Moreover the map

\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})(X)\to\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})(X^{\prime})

associated to
m
corresponds to the map

\displaystyle{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}})

\displaystyle\to{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\varphi\mapsto({\mathcal{F}}\xrightarrow{\varphi}{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}}\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{Id}}\otimes\phi_{m}}{\mathcal{G}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})

where
\phi_{m}:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is dual to
m
via
[
StP
,
Tag 081V
]
.

Proof.

Note that
m
and
f
are flat and finitely presented. The following identifications prove the first assertion

\displaystyle\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})(X)

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(f^{*}{\mathcal{F}},f^{*}{\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},f_{*}f^{*}{\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}}).

The second assertion also follows from these intermediate identifications.
∎

Proposition 9.10

Let
H={\operatorname{Spec}}_{B}({\mathcal{A}})
be a monoid algebraic space over
B
such that
H\to B
is affine, flat and finitely presented. We assume that the multiplication
H\times_{B}H\to H
is flat. An
H
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module structure (over
B_{spaces,fppf}
) on a quasi-coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module
{\mathcal{F}}
corresponds to an
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-comodule structure on
{\mathcal{F}}
, i.e a morphism of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-modules
{\mathcal{F}}\xrightarrow{\delta}{\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}}
such that
(\delta\otimes{\operatorname{Id}}_{\mathcal{A}})\circ\delta=({\operatorname{Id}}_{{\mathcal{F}}}\otimes\Delta_{{\mathcal{A}}})\circ\delta
and
({\operatorname{Id}}_{{\mathcal{F}}}\otimes\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}})\circ\delta={\operatorname{Id}}_{{\mathcal{F}}}
.

Proof.

Let us first remark that the set of
H
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module structures on an
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module
{\mathcal{F}}
is a subset of the set
Mor_{B_{spaces,fppf}}(H,\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B,fppf}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}))
. Using Proposition
9.9
we get

\displaystyle Mor_{B_{spaces,fppf}}(H,\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}))

\displaystyle=\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}})(H)

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}}).

The condition that
{\mathcal{F}}
is an
H
-module means that the transformations of functors

T_{1}:H\times H\xrightarrow{m}H\xrightarrow{\Psi}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}})

and

T_{2}:H\times H\xrightarrow{\Psi\times\Psi}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}})\times\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{\circ}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}})

are equal and that the composition

e_{B}\xrightarrow{}H\xrightarrow{\Psi}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}})

is the identity.
The transformations
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
correspond to elements in

{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{A}}),

namely
T_{1}
corresponds to
({\operatorname{Id}}_{{\mathcal{F}}}\otimes\Delta_{{\mathcal{A}}})\circ\delta
(cf. Proposition
9.9
) and
T_{2}
corresponds to
(\delta\otimes{\operatorname{Id}}_{{\mathcal{A}}})\circ\delta
. The composition

e_{B}\xrightarrow{}H\xrightarrow{\Psi}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}})

corresponds to an element in
{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{O}}_{B})
, namely
({\operatorname{Id}}_{{\mathcal{F}}}\otimes\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}})\circ\delta
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 9.11

An
A(M)_{B}
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module (over
B_{spaces,fppf}
) structure on a quasi-coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module
{\mathcal{F}}
corresponds to a collection
(\mu_{m})_{m\in M}\in{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}})
such that for all
m,k\in M
,

\mu_{m}\circ\mu_{k}=\mu_{k}\text{ if }k=m,~\mu_{m}\circ\mu_{k}=0\text{ if }k\neq m\text{ and }\sum_{m\in M}\mu_{m}={\operatorname{Id}}_{{\mathcal{F}}}.

Proof.

Proposition
9.10
shows that an
A(M)_{B}
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module corresponds to a morphism of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-comodule

\mu:{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{O}}_{B}[M]=\bigoplus_{m\in M}{\mathcal{F}}.

Now
\mu
gives a collection
(\mu_{m})_{m\in M}
with
\mu_{m}\in{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}})
. Saying that
\mu
is a comodule is equivalent to the conditions in the statement (cf. Fact.
2.2
and Def.
2.3
for the
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-coalgebra structure on the quasi-coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-algebra of the affine monoid algebraic space
A(M)_{B}
, namely
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}[M]
). ∎

Proposition 9.12

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a quasi-coherent
A(M)_{B}
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module over
B_{spaces,fppf}
. Let
V\in B_{spaces,fppf}
. We have

\underline{C^{n}(A(M)_{B},{\mathcal{F}})}(V)=\Big(\bigoplus_{(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in M^{n}}{\mathcal{F}}X^{m_{1}}\cdots X^{m_{n}}\Big)(V)

where for every
m=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in M^{n}
,
{\mathcal{F}}X^{m_{1}}\cdots X^{m_{n}}={\mathcal{F}}^{m}
is a formal copy of
{\mathcal{F}}
. In particular if
V
is quasi-compact we have

\underline{C^{n}(A(M)_{B},{\mathcal{F}})}(V)=\bigoplus_{m\in M^{n}}{\mathcal{F}}^{m}(V)

Moreover for any
n\geqslant 0
, the linear map
\partial:C^{n}\to C^{n+1}
is given on the component
{\mathcal{F}}X^{m_{1}}\cdots X^{m_{n}}
and on quasi-compact objects by the formula

\displaystyle f^{m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}}X^{m_{1}}\cdots X^{m_{n}}\mapsto

\displaystyle\mu(f^{m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}})X^{m_{1}}\cdots X^{m_{n}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}f^{m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}}

\displaystyle X^{m_{1}}\cdots\Delta(X^{m_{i}})\cdots X^{m_{n}}

\displaystyle+(-1)^{n+1}f^{m_{1},\cdots,m_{n}}

\displaystyle X^{m_{1}}\ldots X^{m_{n}}X^{0}

where
\Delta(X^{m_{i}})=X^{m_{i}}X^{m_{i}}
.

Proof.

Put
p:A(M)_{V}^{n}\to V
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{V}={\mathcal{F}}|_{V}
. The identifications

\displaystyle\underline{C^{n}(A(M)_{B},{\mathcal{F}})}(V)

\displaystyle=Mor_{V_{spaces,fppf}}(A(M)_{V}^{n},{\mathcal{F}}_{V})

\displaystyle=Mor_{V_{spaces,fppf}}(A(M)_{V}^{n},\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{V}}({\mathcal{O}}_{V},{\mathcal{F}}_{V}))

\displaystyle=\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{V}}({\mathcal{O}}_{V},{\mathcal{F}}_{V})(A(M)_{V}^{n})

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{V}}({\mathcal{O}}_{V},{\mathcal{F}}_{V}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{V}}{\mathcal{O}}_{V}[M]\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{V}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{V}}{\mathcal{O}}_{V}[M])

\displaystyle=\Big({\mathcal{F}}_{V}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{V}}{\mathcal{O}}_{V}[M]\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{V}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{V}}{\mathcal{O}}_{V}[M]\Big)(V)

\displaystyle=\Big({\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{O}}_{B}[M]\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}{\mathcal{O}}_{B}[M]\Big)(V)

\displaystyle=\Big(\bigoplus_{(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in M^{n}}{\mathcal{F}}X^{m_{1}}\cdots X^{m_{n}}\Big)(V)

prove the first assertion. The second assertion is now a consequence of Definition
9.5
.
∎

Proposition 9.13

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a quasi-coherent
A(M)_{B}
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
-module over
B_{spaces,fppf}
, then

H^{1}_{monoid}(A(M)_{B},{\mathcal{F}})=0.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that
Z^{1}_{monoid}(A(M)_{B},{\mathcal{F}})\subset B^{1}(A(M)_{B},{\mathcal{F}})
.
Let
U
be a quasi-compact object in
B_{spaces,fppf}
. Put
F=\Gamma(U,{\mathcal{F}})
and
R=\Gamma(U,{\mathcal{O}}_{B})
.
Let
\xi_{U}=\sum_{m\in M}f_{m}X^{m}
be an element in
\Gamma(U,{\mathcal{F}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S}[M])=\bigoplus_{m\in M}FX^{m}
. Assume that
\partial(\xi_{U})=0
, then

0=\Big(\sum_{(k,m)\in M^{2}}\mu_{k}(f_{m})X^{(k,m)}\Big)-\Big(\sum_{l\in M}f_{l}X^{(l,l)}\Big)+\Big(\sum_{n\in M}f_{n}X^{(n,0)}\Big).

So
\mu_{k}(f_{0})=-f_{k}
for all
k\in M
with
k\neq 0
and

\mu_{0}(f_{0})=0
.
Put
e_{U}:=-f_{0}\in F=\Gamma(U,{\mathcal{F}})
. We have

\partial(e_{U})=\Big(\sum_{k\in M}\mu_{k}(e_{U})X^{k}\Big)-e_{U}X^{0}=\sum_{0\neq k\in M}f_{k}X^{k}+f_{0}X^{0}=\xi_{U}.

So
\xi_{U}
belongs to the image of
\Gamma(U,{\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{\partial}\Gamma(U,{\mathcal{F}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{B}[M])
. Now let
\xi\in\Gamma(B,{\mathcal{F}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{B}[M])
and assume
\partial(\xi)=0
, i.e.
\xi\in Z^{1}_{monoid}(A(M)_{B},{\mathcal{F}})
. Let us consider the set
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of all quasi-compact objects in
B_{spaces,fppf}
, of course it provides an fppf covering of
B
. For each
i\in I
,
\partial(\xi|_{U_{i}})=0
, so the above computation gives us elements
e_{i}\in\Gamma(U_{i},{\mathcal{F}})
such that
\partial(e_{i})=\xi|_{U_{i}}
. These
e_{i}
are compatible and there exists an element
e\in\Gamma(B,{\mathcal{F}})
such that
e|_{U_{i}}=e_{i}
for all
i\in I
. We have
\partial(e)=\xi
. So
\xi\in B^{1}_{monoid}(A(M)_{B},{\mathcal{F}})
. This finishes the proof.

∎

Remark 9.14
.

We conjecture that our method will lead to a proof that
H^{n}_{monoid}(A(M)_{B},{\mathcal{F}})=0
also for
n\geqslant 2
. Note that
[
SGA3
, Exp I. Théorème 5.3.3]
proves that
H^{n}(D(M)_{S},{\mathcal{F}})=0
(
n\geqslant 1
) under the assumption that
S
is affine and that
{\mathcal{F}}
is quasi-coherent, using derived functors. Our computational method, highly inspired by formulas given –and unexploited– in
[
SGA3
]
, shows that the assumption that
S
is affine is unnecessary in
[
SGA3
, Exp. I Théorème 5.3.3]
(at least in the case
n=1
).

10
Equivariant infinitesimal deformations

Let
S
be a scheme. Let
G
be a group algebraic space over
S
.

Proposition 10.1

Let
X,X^{\prime},Y
and
Z
be
S
-algebraic spaces endowed with
S
-actions of
G
. Let

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{Z}

be a commutative diagram of
G
-equivariant
S
-morphisms where the left vertical morphism
i
is a first order thickening, cf.
[
StP
,
Tag 05ZK
]
.
Assume that
Y\to Z
is formally smooth and

H^{1}_{monoid}(G,p_{*}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}}))=0,

where
p_{*}:Sh(X^{\prime}_{spaces,fppf})\to Sh(S_{spaces,fppf})
(cf.
[
StP
,
Tag 00X6
]
for
p_{*}
, §
9.2
for
H^{1}_{monoid}(-,-)
and
[
StP
,
Tag 04CN
]
for
\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}}
). Then there exists a
G
-equivariant morphism
\phi:X^{\prime}\to Y
filling the diagram, i.e. such that
\phi\circ i=a
and
f\circ\phi=\varphi.

Proof.

For an object
U^{\prime}
of
(X^{\prime})_{spaces,fppf}
with
U=X\times_{X^{\prime}}U^{\prime}
, consider morphisms
a^{\prime}:U^{\prime}\to Y
such that

(1)
a^{\prime}
is a morphism over
Z
, and

(2)
a^{\prime}|_{U}=a|_{U}
.

Then the arguments of
[
StP
,
Tag 061A
]
show that the rule
U^{\prime}\mapsto\{a^{\prime}:U^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
such that (1) and (2) hold.
\}
defines a sheaf of sets
\mathcal{P}
on
(X^{\prime})_{spaces,fppf}
. Note that the condition (3) in
[
StP
, 061A]
is empty because
Y=Y^{\prime}
in our situation. By
[
StP
,
Tag 061C
]
, there is an action of the sheaf of abelian groups
\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}})

on the sheaf
\mathcal{P}
.
Moreover, the action of
\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}})
on
\mathcal{P}
is simply transitive
for any object
U^{\prime}
of
(X^{\prime})_{spaces,fppf}

over which the sheaf
\mathcal{P}
has a section. Let
p:X^{\prime}\to S
be the structural morphism. By
[
StP
,
Tag 00X6
]
,
p
induces a morphism of topoi
p_{*}

\displaystyle p_{*}:Sh(X^{\prime}_{spaces,fppf})

\displaystyle\to Sh(S_{spaces,fppf})

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}~~~~~~

\displaystyle\mapsto\big((T\to S)\mapsto\mathcal{F}(X^{\prime}\times_{S}T)\big).

The action of
\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}}))

on
\mathcal{P}
induces an action of the sheaf of abelian groups
p_{*}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}})

on the sheaf
p_{*}\mathcal{P}
. We want to show that
G
acts on the sheaf of abelian groups
p_{*}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}})
and
on the sheaf
p_{*}\mathcal{P}
, in a compatible way. Let
T
be an algebraic space over
S
and let
g\in G(T)
.
An element
a^{\prime}\in(p_{*}\mathcal{P})(T)
is by definition an
S
-morphism
X^{\prime}\times_{S}T\to Y
such that
a^{\prime}
is an
S
-morphism over
Z
and
a^{\prime}\mid_{X\times_{S}T}=a\mid_{X\times_{S}T}
. In other words, an element
a^{\prime}\in(p_{*}\mathcal{P})(T)
is by definition a
T
-morphism
X^{\prime}\times_{S}T\to Y\times_{S}T

such that
a^{\prime}
is a
T
-morphism over
Z\times_{S}T
and
a^{\prime}\mid_{X\times_{S}T}=a\mid_{X\times_{S}T}
. Let
g^{-1}
be the inverse of
g
in
G(T)
. As usual,
X^{\prime}_{T},X_{T},Y_{T}
and
Z_{T}
denote
X^{\prime}\times_{S}T,X\times_{S}T,Y\times_{S}T
and
Z\times_{S}T
. The element
g^{-1}
gives us elements
g^{-1}_{X^{\prime}_{T}}\in{\operatorname{Aut}}_{T}(X^{\prime}_{T})
and
g^{-1}_{X_{T}}\in{\operatorname{Aut}}_{T}(X_{T})
. The element
g
gives us an element
g_{Y_{T}}\in{\operatorname{Aut}}_{T}(Y_{T})
and an element
g_{Z_{T}}\in{\operatorname{Aut}}_{T}(Z_{T})
. For any
a^{\prime}\in(p_{*}\mathcal{P})(T)
, we put
g\cdot a^{\prime}:=g_{Y_{T}}\circ a^{\prime}\circ g^{-1}_{X^{\prime}_{T}}
, this is a
T
-morphism
X^{\prime}_{T}\to Y_{T}
. Let us check that
g\cdot a^{\prime}
belongs to
(p_{*}\mathcal{P})(T)
. Since
a
is
G
equivariant we have
a=g_{Y_{T}}\circ a\circ g^{-1}_{X_{T}}
, this shows that
g\cdot a^{\prime}\mid_{X_{T}}=a\mid_{X_{T}}
. Let us now show that
g\cdot a^{\prime}
is a
T
-morphism over
Z\times_{S}T
. Let
f_{T}
and
\phi_{T}
denote the base change from
S
to
T
of
f
and
\phi
. We have

\displaystyle f_{T}\circ g_{Y_{T}}\circ a^{\prime}\circ g^{-1}_{X^{\prime}_{T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{because }f_{T}\text{ is equivariant}

\displaystyle=g_{Z_{T}}\circ f_{T}\circ a^{\prime}\circ g^{-1}_{X^{\prime}_{T}}

\displaystyle\text{because }a^{\prime}\in(p_{*}\mathcal{P})(T)

\displaystyle=g_{Z_{T}}\circ\varphi_{T}\circ g^{-1}_{X^{\prime}_{T}}

\displaystyle\text{because }\varphi_{T}\text{ is equivariant}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{T}.

This finishes the verification that
g\cdot a^{\prime}\in(p_{*}\mathcal{P})(T)
. We now define the action of
G
on

p_{*}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}})

giving the action of
g\in G(T)
on
v\in(p_{*}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}}))(T)
. The diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.16371pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.16371pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-15.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{T}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 11.31746pt\raise 5.85695pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.15695pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g_{Y_{T}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.16371pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 34.16371pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.32742pt\raise-15.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{T}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.32742pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-9.92065pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Z_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 11.54489pt\raise-25.47636pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.15695pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g_{Z_{T}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.40677pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 34.40677pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Z_{T}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

whose horizontal arrows are isomorphisms, is commutative since
f_{T}
is equivariant. So we obtain an automorphism
g_{\Omega_{T}}
of
\Omega_{Y_{T}/Z_{T}}
, e.g. by
[
StP
,
Tag 04CX
]
. Note that
g_{\Omega_{T}}\circ h_{\Omega_{T}}=(hg)_{\Omega_{T}}
, e.g. by
[
StP
,
Tag 05Z7
]
. That induces similar automorphisms of
a_{T}^{*}\Omega_{Y_{T}/Z_{T}}
. Similarly, using
[
StP
,
Tag 04CP
]
and
[
StP
,
Tag 04G2
]
, we get for any
g,h\in G(T)
automorphisms
g_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}
of
\mathcal{C}_{X_{T}/X^{\prime}_{T}}
such that
g_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}\circ h_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}=(hg)_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}
. Now let
v\in{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a_{T}^{*}\Omega_{Y_{T}/Z_{T}},\mathcal{C}_{X_{T}/X^{\prime}_{T}})=(p_{*}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}}))(T)
and
g\in G(T)
. We put
g\cdot v=g^{-1}_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}\circ v\circ g_{\Omega_{T}}
. The element
g\cdot v
belongs to
(p_{*}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}}))(T)
. This defines a left action because

h\cdot(g\cdot v)=h^{-1}_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}\circ g^{-1}_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}\circ v\circ g_{\Omega_{T}}\circ h_{\Omega_{T}}=(g_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}\circ h_{\mathcal{C}_{T}})^{-1}\circ v\circ g_{\Omega_{T}}\circ h_{\Omega_{T}}=(hg)^{-1}_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}\circ v\circ hg_{\Omega_{T}}=hg\cdot v.

Using
[
StP
,
Tag 0618
]
,
we obtain that the actions are compatible in the sense that

g\cdot a^{\prime}+g\cdot v=g\cdot(a^{\prime}+v)\text{ for any }g\in G(T),a^{\prime}\in(p_{*}\mathcal{P})(T),v\in(p_{*}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}}))(T).

We now define an element
c
in
Z^{1}_{monoid}(G,(p_{*}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}})))
. Let us fix
a^{\prime}\in(p_{*}\mathcal{P})(S)
, this is possible because
Y\to Z
is formally smooth. For any
T\to S\in S_{spaces,fppf}
, put
c(T):G(T)\to(p_{*}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}}))(T)
,
g\mapsto g\cdot a^{\prime}_{T}-a^{\prime}_{T}
. Then
c
is indeed a cocycle because for any
T\in S_{spaces,fppf}
and
g,g^{\prime}\in G(T)
we have

c(g)+g\cdot c(g^{\prime})=g\cdot a^{\prime}_{T}-a^{\prime}_{T}+g\cdot(g^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}_{T}-a^{\prime}_{T})=gg^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}_{T}-a^{\prime}_{T}=c(gg^{\prime}).

By assumption,
H^{1}_{monoid}(G,(p_{*}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}})))=0
, so there is an element

v\in(p_{*}\mathit{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}(a^{*}\Omega_{Y/Z},\mathcal{C}_{X/X^{\prime}}))(S)

such that
c(g)=g\cdot v_{T}-v_{T}
for all
T\in S_{spaces,fppf}
and all
g\in G(T)
.
So for any
T\in S_{spaces,fppf}
and any
g\in G(T)
, we have

g\cdot a^{\prime}_{T}-a^{\prime}_{T}=g\cdot v_{T}-v_{T}
, so we have
g\cdot(a^{\prime}_{T}-v_{T})=a^{\prime}_{T}-v_{T}
. We now put
\phi:=a^{\prime}-v\in(p_{*}\mathcal{P})(S)=\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})
. Then
\phi
is a morphism from
X^{\prime}
to
Y
satisfying all the required properties, this finishes the proof.

∎

Remark 10.2
.

The structure of the proof of Proposition
10.1
is partly similar to the argument sketched in
[
SGA3
, Exp. XII proof of Lemma 9.4]
(unpublished), though frameworks are different.

11
Formal étaleness, formal smoothness and formal unramifiedness

Let
S
be a scheme and let
M
be a finitely generated abelian group. Let
X\to Y
be a
D(M)_{S}
-equivariant morphism of algebraic spaces over
S
. Let
N\subset M
be a submonoid.

Proposition 11.1

Assume that
f:X\to Y
is formally smooth and locally of finite presentation (i.e. smooth by
[
StP
,
Tag 04AM
]
), then
f^{N}:X^{N}\to Y^{N}
is formally smooth as transformation of functors (cf. e.g.
[
StP
,
Tag 049S
]
).

Proof.

Let
\iota_{T}:\overline{T}\to T
be a first order thickening of affine schemes, over
S
. Let
D
:

\textstyle{\overline{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{T}}
\textstyle{X^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{N}}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{Y^{N}}

be a commutative diagram of
S
-functors. We have to prove that there exists an
S
-morphism
\phi:T\to X^{N}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{\overline{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{T}}
\textstyle{X^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{N}}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{Y^{N}}

commutes.
The diagram
D
corresponds to the following data (i), (ii)

1.

\overline{\phi}
is a
D(M)_{\overline{T}}
-equivariant
\overline{T}
-morphism
A(N)_{\overline{T}}\to X_{\overline{T}}

2.

\varphi
is a
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant
T
-morphism
A(N)_{T}\to Y_{T}

such that
\varphi|_{\overline{T}}:A(N)_{\overline{T}}\to Y_{\overline{T}}
equals the map
A(N)_{\overline{T}}\xrightarrow{\overline{\phi}}X_{\overline{T}}\xrightarrow{f_{\overline{T}}}Y_{\overline{T}}
. Let us now consider the commutative diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 18.35121pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.9514pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A(N)_{\overline{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.81796pt\raise 5.83888pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.35121pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 42.35121pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X_{\overline{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.92064pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 86.92064pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.60518pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{T}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.60518pt\raise-24.66669pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-18.35121pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A(N)_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.17271pt\raise-26.8125pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varphi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.44147pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 49.63593pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 87.44147pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y_{T}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

The spaces
A(N)_{\overline{T}}
and
X_{\overline{T}}
are canonically endowed with actions of
D(M)_{T}
, moreover all arrows in the diagram are
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant. By Propositions
10.1
and
9.13
, we get a
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant map
\phi:A(N)_{T}\to X_{T}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{A(N)_{\overline{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}}
\textstyle{X_{\overline{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{T}}
\textstyle{A(N)_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{Y_{T}}

is commutative. The map
\phi
corresponds to a morphism
T\to X^{N}
and satisfies all the required properties to make the diagram

\textstyle{\overline{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{T}}
\textstyle{X^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{N}}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{Y^{N}}

commutative.
∎

Corollary 11.2

Assume that
X\to Y
is smooth and that
X^{N}
and
Y^{N}
are representable by algebraic spaces. Then
X^{N}\to Y^{N}
is smooth as algebraic spaces.

Proof.

Trivial by Propositions
3.19
and
11.1
, and
[
StP
,
Tag 060G
]
.
∎

Proposition 11.3

Assume that
f:X\to Y
is formally unramified, then
f^{N}:X^{N}\to Y^{N}
is formally unramified as transformation of functors (cf. e.g.
[
StP
,
Tag 049S
]
).

Proof.

Let
\iota_{T}:\overline{T}\to T
be a first order thickening of affine schemes, over
S
. Let
D
:

\textstyle{\overline{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{T}}
\textstyle{X^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{N}}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{Y^{N}}

be a commutative diagram of
S
-functors. We have to prove that there exists a most one
S
-morphism
\phi:T\to X^{N}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{\overline{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{T}}
\textstyle{X^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{N}}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{Y^{N}}

commutes. Let
\phi_{a},\phi_{b}
be two such morphisms, we have to prove that
\phi_{a}=\phi_{b}
.
The diagram
D
corresponds to the following data (i), (ii)

1.

\overline{\phi}
is a
D(M)_{\overline{T}}
-equivariant
\overline{T}
-morphism
A(N)_{\overline{T}}\to X_{\overline{T}}

2.

\varphi
is a
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant
T
-morphism
A(N)_{T}\to Y_{T}

such that
\varphi|_{\overline{T}}:A(N)_{\overline{T}}\to Y_{\overline{T}}
equals the map
A(N)_{\overline{T}}\xrightarrow{\overline{\phi}}X_{\overline{T}}\xrightarrow{f_{\overline{T}}}Y_{\overline{T}}
. Let us now consider the commutative diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 18.35121pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.9514pt\raise 97.60518pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{T}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y_{T}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

The morphisms
\phi_{a},\phi_{b}
provide two diagonals of the diagram above. Now using that
f_{T}
if formally unramified, we have
\phi_{a}=\phi_{b}
.
∎

Corollary 11.4

Assume that

is formally unramified and that
X^{N}
and

are representable by algebraic spaces. Then

is formally unramified as morphism of algebraic spaces.

Proof.

Trivial by Proposition
11.3
and
[
StP
,
Tag 04G7
]
.
∎

Proposition 11.5

Assume that
f:X\to Y
is formally étale and locally of finite presentation (i.e étale by
[

]
), then

is formally étale as transformation of functors (cf. e.g.

,

]
).

Proof.

This follows from
[
StP
,
Tag 049S
]
and Propositions
11.3
and

.
∎

Corollary 11.6

Assume that

is étale and that

and

.
