Proposition 11.7

Assume that
f:X\to S
is smooth, let
F
be a face of
N
, then
f^{N,F}:X^{N}\to X^{F}
is formally smooth as transformation of functors (cf. e.g.
[
StP
,
Tag 049S
]
).

Proof.

Let
\iota_{T}:\overline{T}\to T
be a first order thickening of affine schemes, over
S
. Let
R
and
I\subset R
such that
T={\operatorname{Spec}}(R)
and
\overline{T}={\operatorname{Spec}}(R/I)
. Let
D
:

\textstyle{\overline{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{T}}
\textstyle{X^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{N,F}}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{X^{F}}

be a commutative diagram of
S
-functors. We have to prove that there exists an
S
-morphism
\phi:T\to X^{N}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{\overline{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{T}}
\textstyle{X^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{N,F}}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{X^{F}}

commutes.
The diagram
D
corresponds to the following data (i), (ii)

1.

\overline{\phi}
is a
D(M)_{\overline{T}}
-equivariant
\overline{T}
-morphism
A(N)_{\overline{T}}\to X_{\overline{T}}

2.

\varphi
is a
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant
T
-morphism
A(F)_{T}\to X_{T}

such that
\varphi|_{\overline{T}}:A(F)_{\overline{T}}\to Y_{\overline{T}}
equals the map
A(F)_{\overline{T}}\to A(N)_{\overline{T}}\xrightarrow{\overline{\phi}}X_{\overline{T}}
. We need to prove the following fact.

Fact 11.8

The push-out of the diagram
A(F)_{T}\leftarrow A(F)_{\overline{T}}\rightarrow A(N)_{\overline{T}}
, in the category of algebraic spaces over
T
, exists and is denoted

A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}.

Moreover
A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}
is an affine scheme over
T
and is in fact a push-out of

{A(F)_{T}\leftarrow A(F)_{\overline{T}}\rightarrow A(N)_{\overline{T}}}

in the category whose objects are algebraic spaces over
T
and whose morphisms are
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant morphisms of algebraic spaces over
T
.

Proof.

By
[
StP
,
Tag 0ET0
]
, the push-out of
A(F)_{T}\leftarrow A(F)_{\overline{T}}\rightarrow A(N)_{\overline{T}}
exists in the category of schemes and is given by

A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}:={\operatorname{Spec}}\big(\bigoplus_{n\in F}R.X^{n}\oplus\bigoplus_{n\in N\setminus F}(R/I).X^{n}\big).

Since
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant morphisms of affine schemes correspond to
M
-graded morphisms, we obtain that
A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}
is endowed with an action of
D(M)_{T}
and that

{\nu_{1}:A(F)_{T}\to A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}}

and

\nu_{2}:A(N)_{\overline{T}}\to A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}

are
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant. By
[
StP
,
Tag 0ET0
]
, the push-out of

D(M)_{T}\times_{T}A(F)_{T}\leftarrow D(M)_{T}\times_{T}A(F)_{\overline{T}}\rightarrow D(M)_{T}\times_{T}A(N)_{\overline{T}}

exists in the category of schemes and the explicit formula allows us to identify it canonically with

D(M)_{T}\times_{T}\big(A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}\big).

We now remark that push-outs in the category of schemes over
T
give push-outs in the full category of algebraic spaces over
T
by
[
StP
,
Tag 07SY
]
. Now let
X
be an algebraic space over
T
with
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant morphisms
f_{1}:A(F)_{T}\to X
and
f_{2}:A(N)_{\overline{T}}\to X
such that the diagram

\textstyle{A(F)_{\overline{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{A(N)_{\overline{T}}^{f_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A(F)_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X}

commutes; we have to prove that the obtained morphism
f_{1}\coprod f_{2}:A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}\to X
is
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant. For this consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(M)_{T}\times_{T}A(F)_{\overline{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(M)_{T}\times_{T}A(N)_{\overline{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(M)_{T}\times A(F)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(M)_{T}\times_{T}\big(A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(M)_{T}\times_{T}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(F)_{\overline{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(N)_{\overline{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(F)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id\times\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id\times i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{A(N)_{\overline{T}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id\times a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id\times f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id\times f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{A(F)_{{T}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id\times a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id\times(f_{1}\coprod f_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}\coprod f_{2}}

where
m_{{\mathcal{S}}}
denote the multiplication morphism for any
D(M)_{T}
-space
{\mathcal{S}}
.
We have to show that
m_{X}\circ\big(Id\times(f_{1}\coprod f_{2})\big)=(f_{1}\coprod f_{2})\circ m_{A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}}
. Since, as we noted before,
D(M)_{T}\times_{T}(A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}})
is the push-out of

D(M)_{T}\times_{T}A(F)_{T}\xleftarrow{Id\times\iota}D(M)_{T}\times_{T}A(F)_{\overline{T}}\xrightarrow{Id\times i}D(M)_{T}\times_{T}A(N)_{\overline{T}},

it is enough to prove that

1.

m_{X}\circ\big(Id\times(f_{1}\coprod f_{2})\big)\circ(Id\times a_{1})=(f_{1}\coprod f_{2})\circ m_{A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}}\circ(Id\times a_{1})

2.

m_{X}\circ\big(Id\times(f_{1}\coprod f_{2})\big)\circ(Id\times a_{2})=(f_{1}\coprod f_{2})\circ m_{A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}}\circ(Id\times a_{2})
.

These identities are easy to check on the diagram using that
a_{1},a_{2},f_{1},f_{2}
are equivariant and that
f_{1}=(f_{1}\coprod f_{2})\circ a_{1}
,
f_{2}=(f_{1}\coprod f_{2})\circ a_{2}
.
∎

We now continue the proof of Proposition
11.7
.
Let us consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(F)_{\overline{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(N)_{\overline{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(F)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(F)_{T}\coprod_{A(F)_{\overline{T}}}A(N)_{\overline{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\overline{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(N)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Phi}}

where

1.

\overline{\Phi}
is the map obtained from the universal property of pushout from
\overline{\phi}
and
\varphi
.

2.

\Psi
is the map obtained from the universal property of pushout from the canonical morphisms
A(N)_{\overline{T}}\to A(N)_{T}
and
A(F)_{T}\to A(N)_{T}
.

By Fact
11.8
,
i
and
\iota
are
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant and so
\Psi
and
\overline{\Phi}
are also
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant. Now we apply Propositions
10.1
and
9.13
to get a
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant morphism
\phi:A(N)_{T}\to X_{T}
such that the right lower quadrigone commutes. The morphism
\phi
satisfies all required properties to make the diagram

\textstyle{\overline{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{T}}
\textstyle{X^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{N,F}}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{X^{F}}

commutative.
∎

Corollary 11.9

Let
F
be a face of a magnet
N
.
Assume that
X\to S
is smooth and that
X^{N}
and
X^{F}
are representable by algebraic spaces. Then
X^{N}\to X^{F}
is smooth as algebraic spaces.

Proof.

Clear by Proposition
11.7
, and
[
StP
,
Tag 060G
]
.
∎

Remark 11.10
.

If
X\to Y
is flat, then
X^{N}\to Y^{N}
is not flat in general (as we know that flatness is not preserved by taking fixed-points, e.g. cf. Vistoli’s answer in Mathoverflow ”Under what hypotheses are schematic fixed points of a flat deformation themselves flat?”).

Remark 11.11
.

If
X
is Cohen-Macaulay, then
X^{N}
is not Cohen-Macaulay in general (as we know that being Cohen-Macaulay is not preserved by taking fixed-points, e.g. cf. Vistoli’s answer in Mathoverflow ”Are schematic fixed-points of a Cohen-Macaulay scheme Cohen-Macaulay?”).

12
Topology and geometry of attractors

Let
M
be a finitely generated abelian group. Let
X
be an
S
-algebraic space locally of
finite presentation endowed with an action of
D(M)_{S}
.

Proposition 12.1

Let
N\subset M
be a submonoid. Assume that
N
is finitely generated and that there exists a
N^{*}
-FPR atlas of
X
. Then the map
X^{N}\to X^{N^{*}}
(which is affine by Proposition
8.3
) has geometrically connected fibers and induces a bijection on the sets of connected components
\pi_{0}(X^{N})\simeq\pi_{0}(X^{N^{*}})
of the underlying spaces.

Proof.

We adapt
[
Ri16
, Cor. 1.12]
. Using Proposition
3.30
we can assume that
N^{*}=0
and that
N
is fine and sharp. Let
K
be a field, and let
x:{\operatorname{Spec}}(K)\to X^{0}
be a point. Let
X^{N}_{x}=X^{N}\times_{X^{0},x}{\operatorname{Spec}}(K)
. We claim that its underlying topological space
|X^{N}_{x}|
is connected. Let
L
be a field and let
y:{\operatorname{Spec}}(L)\to X^{N}_{x}
be a point, and denote by
x_{L}
the composition
{\operatorname{Spec}}(L)\to{\operatorname{Spec}}(K)\xrightarrow{x}X^{0}.
Then
x_{L}
and
x
define the same point of
|X^{N}_{x}|
.
Recall that we have a natural action of the monoid scheme
A(N)_{S}
on the attractor
X^{N}
. The
A(N)_{L}
orbit of
y
defines a map
h:A(N)_{L}\to X^{N}_{x}
with
h(1)=y
and
h(0)=x_{L}
. Since
N
is sharp,
L[N]
is integral.
So
A(N)_{L}
is connected. So
x
and
y
lie in the connected set
|h|(|A(N)_{L}|)
. Since
y
was arbitrary, this shows that
|X^{N}_{x}|
is connected. So the continous map
|X^{N}|\to|X^{0}|
has connected fibers, and the assertion on connected components follows from the existence of a continous section
|X^{0}|\subset|X^{N}|
.
∎

Proposition 12.2

Let
N\subset M
be a magnet. Let
f:X\to Y
be a
D(M)_{S}
-equivariant morphism of
S
-algebraic spaces such that
X^{N}
and
Y^{N}
are representable by
S
-algebraic spaces. Let
f^{N}:X^{N}\to Y^{N}
be the canonical morphism obtained on attractors. The following assertions hold.

1.

If
f
is an open immersion, then
f^{N}
is an open immersion.

2.

If the following conditions hold

(a)

f
is a closed immersion,

(b)

there exists a
N^{*}
-FPR atlas of
Y
,

(c)

N
is finitely generated or
X
is separated,

then
f^{N}
is a closed immersion, moreover
X^{N}\cong Y^{N}\times_{Y}X
.

Proof.

1.

Since
f
is an open immersion, it is smooth and a monomorphism. By Corollary
11.2
,
f^{N}
is smooth and in particular locally finitely presented and flat (cf.
[
StP
,
Tag 04TA
]
). By Fact
3.18
,
f^{N}
is a monomorphism. Therefore, by
[
StP
,
Tag 05VH
]
,
f^{N}
is universally injective and unramified. Consequently, by
[
StP
,
Tag 06LU
]
,
f^{N}
is étale. Finally, by
[
StP
,
Tag 05W5
]
,
f^{N}
is an open immersion.

2.

Assume
f
is a closed immersion. The canonical morphisms
X^{N}\to Y^{N}
and
X^{N}\to X
induces a canonical morphism
i:X^{N}\to Y^{N}\times_{Y}X
. We are going to prove that
i
is an isomorphism. Let
U
be a
N^{*}
-FPR atlas of
Y
.
By Proposition
6.7
, the map
U\times_{Y}X\to X
is a
N^{*}
-FPR atlas of
X
. So by Proposition
5.1
the canonical map
(U\times_{Y}X)^{N}\to X^{N}
is étale and surjective. The canonical map
U^{N}\to Y^{N}
(étale and surjective) induces an étale and surjective map
(U\times_{Y}X)\times_{U}U^{N}=X\times_{Y}U^{N}\to X\times_{Y}Y^{N}
.
So we get a diagram

\textstyle{(U\times_{Y}X)^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(U\times_{Y}X)\times_{U}U^{N}=X\times_{Y}U^{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\times_{Y}Y^{N}.}

The left arrow is an isomorphism by Lemma
3.25
(write
U
as a disjoint union of stable
S
-affine schemes
U_{i}
and use that
U_{i}\times_{Y}X\to U_{i}\times_{Y}Y=U_{i}
is affine for all
i
). The horizontal arrows are étale and surjective. The diagram is a cartesian square because

(X\times_{Y}U^{N})\times_{X\times_{Y}Y^{N}}X^{N}=U^{N}\times_{Y^{N}}X^{N}=(U\times_{Y}X)^{N}

by Proposition
3.7
. So by
[
StP
,
Tag 03I2
]
we obtain
X^{N}\cong X\times_{Y}Y^{N}
.

∎

Proposition 12.3

Let
N
be a magnet of
M
and assume
N
is fine as monoid. Let
F
be a face of
N
. Let
X^{F}\xrightarrow{\iota}X^{N}
and
X^{N}\xrightarrow{r}X^{F}
be the associated morphisms (cf.
3.10
and
3.15
).
Then
\iota,r
is a strong deformation retract. That is there exists a morphism
\delta:X^{N}\times_{S}{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{S}\to X^{N}
such that

1.

\delta\circ j_{0}=\iota\circ r
where
j_{0}:X^{N}\to X^{N}\times_{S}{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{S}
is given by the section
0
of
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{S}
,

2.

\delta\circ j_{1}=Id_{X^{N}}
where
j_{1}:X^{N}\to X^{N}\times_{S}{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{S}
is given by the section
1
of
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{S}
,

3.

\delta\circ(\iota\times Id_{{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{S}})=\iota\circ pr_{1}
where
pr_{1}:X^{F}\times_{S}{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{S}\to X^{F}
is the projection.

Proof.

Let
f:A(N)_{S}\times_{S}{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{S}\to A(N)_{S}
be a map as in
[
Og
, Prop. 3.3.1 (iii) p. 75]
. Note that
f
is
D(M)_{S}
-equivariant, i.e.
f(g\cdot x,y)=g\cdot f(x,y)
by construction of
f
in
[
Og
, Proof of Prop. 3.3.1 (iii) p. 75]
. We now define
\delta
using
f
. Let
T/S
be a scheme and let us take an element
x
in
(X^{N}\times_{S}{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{S})(T)
. This element
x
corresponds to a pair of morphisms
(b,c)
where
b:A(N)_{T}\to X_{T}
is
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant and
c:T\to{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{T}
belongs to
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{T}(T).

Let
c^{\prime}
be the composition

c^{\prime}:A(N)_{T}=A(N)_{T}\times_{T}T\xrightarrow{Id\times c}A(N)_{T}\times_{T}{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{T}\xrightarrow{f}A(N)_{T}.

The morphism
c^{\prime}:A(N)_{T}\to A(N)_{T}
is
D(M)_{T}
equivariant.
Now we define
\delta(x)
as the morphism
T\to X^{N}
corresponding to the
D(M)_{T}
-equivariant composition
\delta(x):A(N)_{T}\xrightarrow{c^{\prime}}A(N)_{T}\xrightarrow{b}X_{T}
. This defines
\delta
as functor.
Now
[
Og
, Prop. 3.3.1 (iii) p. 75]
implies that (i), (ii) and (iii) of Proposition
12.3
hold.
∎

The following theorem generalizes the Bialynicki-Birula decomposition.

Theorem 12.4

Assume that
X
is smooth over
S
and that the action
a
of
D(M)_{S}
on
X
is Zariski locally linearizable (cf. §
7
). Assume that
N
is finitely generated as monoid or that
X
is quasi-compact over
S
.
Then the morphism
X^{N}\to X^{N^{*}}
is an affine space fibration.

Proof.

Since the action
a
is Zariski locally linearizable, we reduce to the case where
X
is affine over
S
. Then
X^{N}
and
X^{N^{*}}
are affine over
S
. Using Proposition
8.5
we reduce to the case where
N
is finitely generated. Using Proposition
3.30
(to
M\to Z=M/N^{*}
and
Y=N/N^{*}
), we then reduce to the case where
N
is fine and sharp, in particular
N^{*}=0
.
By
3.12

A(N)_{S}
acts on
X^{N}
. So there exists a quasi-coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-algebra
A=\bigoplus_{n\in N}{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
graded by the fine and sharp monoid
N
such that
X^{N}={\operatorname{Spec}}_{S}(\cal A)
. By
3.31
, we have
X^{0}=(X^{N})^{0}
. Since
N
is sharp, the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
generated by
\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n}|n\in N\setminus 0\}
identifies with
\bigoplus_{n\in N\setminus 0}{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
and
{\mathcal{A}}/{\mathcal{I}}={\mathcal{A}}_{0}
. Then by Theorem
3.20
,
X^{0}={\operatorname{Spec}}_{S}({\mathcal{A}}_{0})
and we have a closed immersion
X^{0}\to X^{N}
. By
11.2
,
X^{N}\to S
and
X^{0}\to S
are smooth, so by
[
StP
,
Tag 067U
]

X^{0}\to X^{N}
is a regular closed immersion. So by
[
StP
,
Tag 063M
]
the conormal sheaf
{\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2}
is finite locally free hence projective. The ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
is graded by definition. The definition of
{\mathcal{I}}
also implies that
{\mathcal{I}}^{2}
is graded.
Therefore, the quotient
map
{\mathcal{I}}\to{\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2}
is a homomorphism of graded
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules. Hence,
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
maps onto

({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})_{n}
for every nonzero
n\in N
. The
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
-module
({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})_{n}
is projective because it is a direct summand of
{\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2}
,
and so the surjection
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\to({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})_{n}
admits a section
{\mathfrak{s}}_{n}:({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})_{n}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{n}.

Let
{\mathfrak{s}}:{\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2}\to{\mathcal{A}}
be the direct sum
\bigoplus_{n\in N}{\mathfrak{s}}_{n}
, note that
{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})\oplus{\mathcal{I}}^{2}={\mathcal{I}}
.
Now
{\mathfrak{s}}
induces a morphism of
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
-algebras
\Phi:Sym_{{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})\to{\mathcal{A}}
. It is enough to show that this morphism is an isomorphism. We note that
Sym_{{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})
is the sheafification of the presheaf
U\mapsto Sym_{{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(U)}({\mathcal{I}}(U)/{\mathcal{I}}^{2}(U))
, cf. e.g.
[
StP
,
Tag 01CG
]
. So, using
[
StP
,
Tag 007V
]
, we reduce to the case where
{\mathcal{A}}
is a ring. We first prove that
\Phi
is surjective.
By
[
Og
, Prop. 2.2.1 p. 34]
, there exists a morphism of monoids
h:N\to{\mathbb{N}}
such that
h^{-1}(0)=0
. The image of
Sym_{{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})\to{\mathcal{A}}
is the
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
-subalgebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}[{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})]
of
{\mathcal{A}}
generated by
{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})
. Let
c\in N
and
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{c}
. We shall prove by induction on
h(c)
that
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{0}[{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})]
.
Certainly this is true if
c=0
. If
h(c)=1
, then
a
belongs to
{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})
and so
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{0}[{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})]
. We now assume that
{\mathcal{A}}_{c}\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{0}[{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})]
for all
c
such that
h(c)\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
with
k\geqslant 1
. Let
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{c}
with
h(c)=k+1
and let us prove that
a
belongs to
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}[{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})]
. There exists
a^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})
such that

a-a^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}^{2}
, and so we may suppose that
a\in{\mathcal{I}}^{2}
. Write
a=\sum_{i}b_{i}d_{i}
with
b_{i}
and
d_{i}
homogeneous elements of
{\mathcal{I}}
. The equality still holds when we omit any terms
b_{i}d_{i}
with
deg(b_{i})+deg(d_{i})\not=c
. For the remaining terms,
h(deg(b_{i})),h(deg(d_{i}))<h(c)
and so
b_{i},d_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{0}[{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})]
. So
\Phi
is surjective. We then prove that
\Phi
is injective.
By
[
StP
,
Tag 062Z
]
and
[
StP
,
Tag 063M
]
(cf. also
[
StP
,
Tag 063H
]
), the surjective morphism
\Theta:Sym_{{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}({\mathcal{I}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{2})\to Gr_{{\mathcal{A}}_{o}}({\mathcal{I}})=\bigoplus_{k\geqslant}{\mathcal{I}}^{k}/{\mathcal{I}}^{k+1}
is an isomorphism. The induced morphism of algebras
\Psi:\bigoplus_{k\geqslant}{\mathcal{I}}^{k}/{\mathcal{I}}^{k+1}\to{\mathcal{A}}
is explicitly given by
\Psi([i_{1}\ldots i_{k}]_{\mathrm{mod}{\mathcal{I}}^{k+1}})\mapsto{\mathfrak{s}}([i_{1}]_{\mathrm{mod}{\mathcal{I}}^{2}})\ldots{\mathfrak{s}}([i_{k}]_{\mathrm{mod}{\mathcal{I}}^{2}})
. Let
\Psi_{k}
be the restriction of
\Psi
to
{\mathcal{I}}^{k}/{\mathcal{I}}^{k+1}
, it factors through
{\mathcal{I}}^{k}
. Let
\pi_{k}
be the reduction modulo
{\mathcal{I}}_{k}
for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then for
d\geqslant k
, the composition
\pi_{d}\circ\Psi_{k}:{\mathcal{I}}^{k}/{\mathcal{I}}^{k+1}\to{\mathcal{A}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{d}
equals
0
if
d\leqslant k
. Moreover, the composition
\pi_{k+1}\circ\Psi_{k}:{\mathcal{I}}^{k}/{\mathcal{I}}^{k+1}\to{\mathcal{I}}^{k}/{\mathcal{I}}^{k+1}
is the identity. We now prove that
\Psi
is injective. So let
X=\sum_{k=0}^{n}i_{k}
with
i_{k}\in{\mathcal{I}}^{k}/{\mathcal{I}}^{k+1}
and
\Psi(X)=0
. We now prove by induction on
j
that
i_{0},i_{1},...,i_{j}
equal zero. We have
0=\Psi(X)\mathrm{mod}{\mathcal{I}}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\Psi(i_{k})\mathrm{mod}{\mathcal{I}}=\Psi(i_{0})\mathrm{mod}{\mathcal{I}}=i_{0}
. So
i_{0}=0
and
X=\sum_{k=1}^{n}i_{k}
. Now assume that
i_{0},i_{1},...,i_{j}
equal zero. We have
0=\Psi(X)\mathrm{mod}{\mathcal{I}}^{j+1}=\sum_{k={j+1}}^{n}\Psi(i_{k})\mathrm{mod}{\mathcal{I}}^{j+1}=\Psi(i_{j+1})\mathrm{mod}{\mathcal{I}}^{j+1}=i_{j+1}
. This shows that
\Psi
and
\Phi
are injective and finishes the proof.
∎

We obtain the following corollary, in the spirit of a classical result on toric varieties over fields (cf. e.g.
[
CLS11
, Theorem 1.3.12, Definition 1.2.16]
).

Corollary 12.5

Let
S
be a scheme. Let
N
be a fine and sharp monoid. If
A(N)_{S}\to S
is smooth, then
A(N)_{S}\to S
is a vector bundle.

Proof.

Let
M
be
N^{gp}
, then
(A(N)_{S})^{N}=A(N)_{S}
and
(A(N)_{S})^{0}=S
. Now Corollary
12.5
follows from the proof of Theorem
12.4
.
∎

13
Attractors and dilatations

Let
S
be a scheme and let
S^{\prime}
be a closed locally principal subscheme of
S
.
Let
X
be an algebraic space over
S
with a
D(M)_{S}
-action where
M
is an abelian group. Put
D=X_{S^{\prime}}
.
Let
Y
be a closed subspace of
X_{S^{\prime}}
.
Then by
[
Ma23
]
(or
[
MRR20
]
if
X
is a scheme), we get a space
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X
called the dilatation of
X
with center
Y
along
S^{\prime}
, and an affine morphism of spaces
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X\to X
. Let
N
be a submonoid in
M
.
Assume that
Y
is stable under the action of
D(M)_{S^{\prime}}
on
X_{S^{\prime}}
. Then by Proposition
12.2

Y^{N}\to D^{N}
is a closed immersion. Moreover
D^{N}=(X_{S^{\prime}})^{N}=(X^{N})_{S^{\prime}}
(cf. Proposition
3.9
) is a locally principal closed subscheme of
X^{N}
. So
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y^{N}}^{D^{N}}X^{N}
is well-defined.

Proposition 13.1

Assume moreover that
X\to S
,
({\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X)^{N}\to S
and
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y^{N}}^{D^{N}}X^{N}\to S
are flat. Then
D(M)_{S}
acts naturally on
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X
, moreover we get a canonical isomorphism

\Theta:({\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X)^{N}\cong{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y^{N}}^{D^{N}}X^{N}.

Remark 13.2
.

We refer to
[
MRR20
, Prop. 2.16]
for conditions ensuring flatness of dilatations.

Proof.

Remark first that since
D(M)_{S}\to S
,
X\to S
,
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X\to S
are flat,
D(M)_{S}
,
X
and
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X
belong to
\mathrm{Spaces}_{S}^{S^{\prime}\text{-}reg}
(cf.
[
Ma23
]
for the definition of
\mathrm{Spaces}_{S}^{S^{\prime}\text{-}reg}
), moreover by flatness any products of these objects in the category
\mathrm{Spaces}/S
or
\mathrm{Spaces}_{S}^{S^{\prime}\text{-}reg}
coincide.
So to check that we have an action of
D(M)_{S}
on
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X
it is enough to show that
D(M)_{S}(T)
acts on
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X(T)
functorially for any
T\in\mathrm{Spaces}_{S}^{S^{\prime}\text{-}reg}
. So let
T\in\mathrm{Spaces}_{S}^{S^{\prime}\text{-}reg}
.
Let
(g,x)\in D(M)_{S}(T)\times{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Z}^{D}(X)(T)
, note that
x
corresponds to a morphism
T\xrightarrow{x}X
such that
T|_{S^{\prime}}\to X_{S^{\prime}}
factors through
Y
. We define
g.x
as the composition

T\xrightarrow{(g,x)}D(M)_{S}\times X\xrightarrow{\text{action}}X.

Then
g.x
restricted to
S^{\prime}
factors through
Y
and so
g.x\in{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Z}^{D}X(T)
. So
D(M)_{S}
acts on
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}(X)
.
We obtain the following two diagrams

({\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X)^{N}\to X^{N},

\hbox to314.29pt{\vbox to51.96pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 157.1419pt\lower-25.98174pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{

\lxSVG@closescope {

{{{{{}}{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

By the universal property of dilatations, we obtain a morphism

\Theta:({\operatorname{Bl}}^{D}_{Y}X)^{N}\to{\operatorname{Bl}}^{D^{N}}_{Y^{N}}X^{N}.

We now prove that it is an isomorphism.
Again, let
T\in\mathrm{Spaces}_{S}^{S^{\prime}-reg}
and let
T^{\prime}
be
T\times_{S}S^{\prime}
. Then
T^{\prime}
is a closed and locally principal subspace of
T
. Moreover, since
A(N)_{T}
is flat over
T
,
A(N)_{T}
belongs to
\mathrm{Spaces}_{T}^{T^{\prime}-reg}
. Since
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X\to S
is flat, by
[
Ma23
, §3.6]
, we have
({\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X)_{T}={\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y_{T^{\prime}}}^{D_{T^{\prime}}}X_{T}
.
So, on the one hand

\displaystyle({\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X)^{N}(T)

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Hom}}^{D(M)_{T}}(A(N)_{T},({\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X)_{T})

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Hom}}^{D(M)_{T}}(A(N)_{T},{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y_{T^{\prime}}}^{D_{T^{\prime}}}X_{T})

\displaystyle=\{A(N)_{T}\xrightarrow{f}X_{T}|~f\text{ is }D(M)_{T}\text{-equiv. and }A(N)_{T}|_{T^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{f_{T^{\prime}}}X|_{T^{\prime}}\text{ factors through }Y_{T^{\prime}}\},

moreover, on the other hand,

\displaystyle({\operatorname{Bl}}^{D^{N}}_{Y^{N}}X^{N})(T)

\displaystyle=\{T\to X^{N}|~~T|_{S^{\prime}}\to X^{N}|_{S^{\prime}}\text{ factors through }Y^{N}\}

\displaystyle=\{A(N)_{T}\xrightarrow{f}X|~f\text{ is }D(M)_{T}\text{-equiv. and }A(N)_{T}|_{T^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{f_{T^{\prime}}}X|_{T^{\prime}}\text{ factors through }Y_{T^{\prime}}\}.

Now since
{\operatorname{Bl}}^{D^{N}}_{Y^{N}}X^{N}
and
({\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X)^{N}
are flat over
S
, they belong to
\mathrm{Spaces}_{S}^{S^{\prime}\text{reg}}
. So by Yoneda
{\operatorname{Bl}}^{D^{N}}_{Y^{N}}X^{N}=({\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X)^{N}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 13.3

Let
S
be a scheme and let
S^{\prime}
be an effective Cartier divisor on
S
.
Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
S
with a
D(M)_{S}
-action where
M
is an abelian group. Put
D=X_{S^{\prime}}
.
Let
Y
be a closed subscheme of
X_{S^{\prime}}
such that
Y\to S^{\prime}
is smooth.
Let
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X
be the dilatation of
X
with center
Y
along
S^{\prime}
. Let
N
be a submonoid in
M
.
Assume that
Y
is stable under the action of
D(M)_{S^{\prime}}
on
X_{S^{\prime}}
. Then
D(M)_{S^{\prime}}
acts on
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X
and we get a canonical isomorphism

\Theta:({\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X)^{N}\cong{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y^{N}}^{D^{N}}X^{N}.

Proof.

Since
X\to S,D\to S^{\prime}
and
Y\to S
are smooth, by Corollary
11.2

X^{N}\to S,D^{N}\to S
and
Y^{N}\to S
are smooth. So by
[
MRR20
, Proposition 2.16]
,
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X\to S
and
{\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y^{N}}^{D^{N}}X^{N}\to S
are smooth. So using Corollary
11.2
again,
({\operatorname{Bl}}_{Y}^{D}X)^{N}\to S
is smooth. Now since smooth implies flat, Corollary
13.3
follows from Proposition
13.1
.
∎

Remark 13.4
.

We note that the fact that dilatations commute with attractors may be used to study valued root data as in Bruhat-Tits theory. Indeed by Section
16.6
root groups of reductive groups are examples of attractors (cf. also Section
16.2
) and dilatations allow to define filtrations.

14
Ind-algebraic spaces

Let
S
be a scheme and let
(Aff/S)
be the category of affine schemes over
S
. Its objects are morphisms
{\operatorname{Spec}}(R)\to S
from affine schemes to
S
. We use
[
HR21
]
for the definition of ind-algebraic spaces.

Definition 14.1
.

An ind-algebraic space (resp. ind-scheme) over
S
is a functor
(Aff/S)\to Set
which admits a presentation
X\cong{\operatorname{colim}}_{i\in I}X_{i}
as a filtered colimit of
S
-algebraic spaces (resp.
S
-schemes) where all transition maps
\phi_{ij}:X_{i}\to X_{j}
,
i\leqslant j
are closed immersions. The category of ind-algebraic spaces (resp. ind-schemes) over
S
is the full subcategory of functors
(Aff/S)\to Set
whose objects are ind-algebraic spaces (resp. ind-schemes) over
S
.

Any algebraic space (resp. scheme) over
S
is naturally an ind-algebraic space (resp an ind-scheme) over
S
. Any ind-scheme over
S
is naturally an ind-algebraic space over
S
.

Remark 14.2
.

[
HR21
, §1.5]
If
X={\operatorname{colim}}_{i}X_{i}
and
Y={\operatorname{colim}}_{j}Y_{j}
are presentations of ind-algebraic spaces
(resp. ind-schemes) over
S
, and if each
X_{i}
is quasi-compact, then as sets

{\operatorname{Hom}}(X,Y)=\lim_{i}{\operatorname{colim}}_{j}{\operatorname{Hom}}(X_{i},Y_{j}),

because every map
X_{i}\to Y
factors over some
Y_{j}
by quasi-compactness of
X_{i}
. The categories of ind-algebraic spaces and ind-schemes are closed under fiber product. If
\mathbf{P}
is a property of algebraic spaces (resp. schemes), then an
S
-ind-algebraic space
(resp.
S
-ind-scheme)
X
is said to have ind-
\mathbf{P}
if there exists a presentation
X={\operatorname{colim}}_{i}X_{i}
where
each
X_{i}
has property
\mathbf{P}.
A map
f:X\to Y
of
S
-ind-algebraic spaces (resp.
S
-ind-schemes) is
said to have property
\mathbf{P}
if
f
is representable and for all schemes
T\to Y,
the pullback
f\times_{Y}T
has
property
\mathbf{P}
. Note that every representable quasi-compact map of
S
-ind-schemes is schematic.

Definition 14.3
.

Let
G
be a group scheme over
S
and let
X
be an ind-algebraic space over
S
.

1.

A categorical action of
G
on
X
is an action of
G
on
X
seen as a functor. Equivalently by Yoneda, a categorical action is a morphism in the category of ind-algebraic spaces over
S

\sigma:G\times_{S}X\to X
satisfying the usual axioms, i.e.

(a)

\sigma\circ({\operatorname{Id}}_{G}\times\sigma)=\sigma\circ(m\times{\operatorname{Id}}_{X})
where
m:G\times_{S}G\to G
is the group law of
G
,

(b)

\sigma\circ(e\times{\operatorname{Id}}_{X})={\operatorname{Id}}_{X}
where
e:S\to G
is the identity section of
G
.

2.

A collection of actions
\sigma_{i}:G\times_{S}X_{i}\to X_{i}
such that for
i\leqslant j
we have
\phi_{ij}\circ\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{j}\circ({\operatorname{Id}}_{G}\times\phi_{ij})
gives birth to a categorical action of
G
on
X
. Such categorical actions are called ind-actions.

Let
M
be a finitely generated abelian group.

Proposition 14.4

Let
X
be an ind-algebraic space (resp. ind-scheme) over
S
. Assume that we have a presentation
X={\operatorname{colim}}_{i}X_{i}
with
X_{i}
quasi-separated and locally finitely presented over
S
. Assume that
D(M)_{S}
acts on
X
via an ind-action on the presentation
X={\operatorname{colim}}_{i}X_{i}
. Let
N\subset M
be a monoid. Assume that
N
is finitely generated or that
X
is separated. Then the attractor functor

(T\to S)\mapsto{\operatorname{Hom}}^{D(M)_{T}}(A(N)_{T},X_{T})

is representable by the ind-algebraic space (resp. ind-scheme)
{\operatorname{colim}}_{i}X_{i}^{N}
. Moreover the natural morphism
X^{N}\to X
is representable by algebraic spaces (resp. schemes). If
N=Z
is a group the natural morphism
X^{Z}\to X
is representable by a closed immersion.

Proof.

The first assertion is a direct corollary of Proposition
12.2
. To show that
X^{N}\to X
, is representable by algebraic spaces, we notice that if
T
is an affine scheme and
T\to X
is
a morphism, then there exists
i
such that this morphism is induced by a morphism
T\to X_{i}
, and
then we have

T\times_{X}X^{N}=T\times_{X_{i}}{X_{i}}^{N}.
If
N=Z
is a group, the last assertion follows from Corollary
8.1
.
∎

15
Pure magnets

Let
M
be an abelian monoid. Let
X
be a separated algebraic space over
S
endowed with an action
a
of
A(M)_{S}
. Let
m(a)
be the set of magnets of
a
, i.e. the set of all submonoids of
M
. We also use the notation
m(M)
to denote
m(a)
.

Proposition 15.1

Let
N\in m(a)
, the following conditions are equivalent.

1.

For any
L\in m(a)
,
L\subsetneq N\Rightarrow X^{L}\subsetneq X^{N}
.

2.

For any
L\in m(a)
,
X^{N}=X^{L}\Rightarrow N\subset L
.

Proof.

Let us prove (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii). So let
L\in m(M)
and assume
X^{N}=X^{L}
. Then
X^{N}=X^{N\cap L}
by Proposition
3.31
.
Since
N\cap L\subset N
, (i) implies that
N\cap L=N
. So
N\subset L
. Reciprocally assume (ii) holds. Let
L\in m(a)
such that
L\subsetneq N
. By Proposition
3.28
, we have
X^{L}\subset X^{N}
. It remains to prove that
X^{L}\neq X^{N}
. This is clear, indeed otherwise (ii) implies that
N\subset L
and so
N\subsetneq N
which is absurd.
∎

Definition 15.2
.

We use the following terminology.

1.

A pure magnet for the action
a
is a magnet
N\in m(a)
satisfying the equivalent properties of Proposition
15.1
.

2.

The set of all pure magnets of the action
a
is denoted
\mho(a)
.

Theorem 15.3

We have a canonical bijection between
\mho(a)
and the set

\{X^{N}\subset X|N\in m(M)\}

with the convention that we identify
X^{N}
and
X^{L}
if and only if
X^{N}(T)=X^{L}(T)\subset X(T)
for all
T/S
(cf. Proposition
3.29
). The bijection sends a pure magnet
N\in\mho(a)
to the attractor
X^{N}
. The reciprocal bijection sends
Y\in\{X^{N}\subset X|N\in m(M)\}
to the pure magnet

E(Y):=\bigcap\big\{N\in m(M)|X^{N}=Y\big\}.

Proof.

Let
Y\in\{X^{N}\subset X|N\in m(M)\}
and let
E(Y)
be the monoid defined in the statement, i.e.
E(Y)=\bigcap\big\{N\in m(M)|X^{N}=Y\big\}
. By Proposition
3.32
, we have
Y=X^{E(Y)}
. Let us prove that
E(Y)
is a pure magnet. Let
L\in m(M)
such that
X^{L}=X^{E(Y)}=Y
, by definition of
E(Y)
, we have
E(Y)\subset L
and so
E(Y)
is a pure magnet. It remains to prove that for all
N\in\mho(a)
and all
Y\in\{X^{N}\subset X|N\in m(M)\}
, we have
E(X^{N})=N
and
Y=X^{E(Y)}
. So let us first take
N\in\mho(a)
. It is obvious that
E(X^{N})\subset N
. On an other hand, by Proposition
3.32
, we have
X^{N}=X^{E(X^{N})}
and so
N\subset E(X^{N})
because
N
is a pure magnet. Now let us take
Y\in\{X^{N}\subset X|N\in m(M)\}
, Proposition
3.32
implies that
Y=X^{E(Y)}
.
∎

Theorem 15.4

Assume moreover that
X
is finitely presented over
S
. Assume additionally that one of the following conditions is satisfied

1.

X
is affine over
S
,

2.

M
is a group and there exists an
S
-affine strongly-FPR atlas for
X
(e.g. the action is Zariski locally linearizable),

3.

add more as needded,

then
\mho(a)
is a finite poset.

Proof.

The set
\mho(a)
is a poset for the inclusion. It remains to show that
\mho(a)
is finite. Assume (i) holds.
Write
X={\operatorname{Spec}}_{S}({\mathcal{A}})
, then we have an
M
-grading
{\mathcal{A}}=\bigoplus_{m\in M}{\mathcal{A}}_{m}
. Because of
X
is finitely presented over
S
, there exists a finite subset
E\subset M
such that
{\mathcal{A}}
is generated locally by homogeneous elements of degree in
E
. Now for any monoid
N
, we have
X^{N}=X^{[N\cap E\rangle}
. This implies that
\mho(a)
is finite. Case (ii) follows from case (i) and the following result:

Proposition 15.5

Let
U\to X
be an
S
-affine strongly-FPR atlas. Let
a_{U}
denote the action of
D(M)_{S}
on
U
. Then for all submonoids
N,L
of
M
, we have
U^{N}=U^{L}\Rightarrow X^{N}=X^{L}
; moreover

\mho(a_{X})\subset\mho(a_{U}).

Proof.

Note that
X^{P}
is representable for any magnet
P
by Proposition
8.3
.
Let
N,L
be submonoids of
M
and assume
U^{N}=U^{L}
. By Proposition
3.31
or Proposition
3.23
, we have
U^{N}=U^{N\cap L}=U^{L}
. So it is enough to prove that
X^{N\cap L}=X^{L}
, in other words we can change notation and assume
N\subset L
. Let us remark that
U\to S
is of finite presentation, indeed
U\to S
being affine, it is quasi-compact and quasi-separated and
U\to X\to S
is locally of finite presentation as compositions of two such morphisms. The map
U^{N}\to X^{N}
is étale and surjective. Indeed since
u
is strongly-FPR, by Proposition
5.1
we get a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{N^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{N^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

with Cartesian squares and
U\to X
is étale and surjective. We get a commutative triangle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
where
f
is surjective and étale,
p
is étale by Corollary
11.6
and
q
is locally of finite presentation (e.g. by
[
StP
,
Tag 05WT
]
and Theorem
8.3
). So
q
is étale by
[
StP
,
Tag 0AHE
]
and in particular unramified. Now since
X
is separated,
q
is a monomorphism by Proposition
3.28
. Being an unramified monomorphism,
q
is universally injective by
[
StP
,
Tag 05W6
]
. So
q
being étale and universally injective, by
[
StP
,
Tag 05W5
]
, it is an open immersion. Now since
p
is surjective, we get that
q
is surjective. So
q
being a surjective open immersion, it is an isomorphism. So we proved that
X^{N}=X^{L}
. Now let
N\in\mho(a_{X})
and let us prove that
N\in\mho(a_{U})
. So let
L\in m(M)
such that
U^{N}=U^{L}
. We proved that
X^{N}=X^{L}
and so
L\subset N
because
N\in\mho(a_{X})
. This finishes the proof.
∎

∎

Remark 15.6
.

Theorem
15.4
shows in particular that, under the same assumptions, the set
\{X^{D(M/Z)_{S}}\subset X\mid Z\subset M
is a subgroup of
M\}
is finite. We did not know a reference for this fact.

Conjecture 15.7

We conjecture that the conclusion of Theorem
15.4
remains true without the additional assumption (i),(ii) or (iii).

Remark 15.8
.

Conjecture
6.9
implies Conjecture
15.7
.

If
N
is a monoid, we denote by
gen(N)
the set of all subsets of
N
that generate
N
as monoid. The emptyset generates the zero monoid.

Definition 15.9
.

The rank
mk(N)
(possibly infinite) of a monoid
N
is the cardinal defined by

mk(N)=\min\{\#E|E\in gen(N)\}.

Understanding combinatorial aspects of
\mho(a)
, including ranks of pure magnets, is a fundamental invariant of the action
a
.

Example 15.10

1.

Let
a
be the trivial action of
A(M)
on a space
X
, then
\mho(a)=\{0\}
.

2.

Let
a
be the action of
D({\mathbb{Z}}^{n})
on itself by multiplication. Then
\mho(a)=\{0,{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\}
.

Fact 15.11

We have
mk({\mathbb{Z}}^{n})=n+1
.

Proof.

Indeed, as monoid,
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
is generated by
\{\{e_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}},-\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\}
. Assume that
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
is generated by
n
elements
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
, then there exists
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}>0
such that
-(f_{1}+\ldots+f_{n})=p_{1}f_{1}+\ldots+p_{n}f_{n}
, this implies
(p_{1}+1)f_{1}+\ldots+(p_{n}+1)f_{n}=0
. So
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
are linked and can not generate
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
as group. This is absurd. So
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
can not be generated by only
n
elements.∎

3.

Let
a
be the action of
A({\mathbb{N}})
on itself by multiplication. Then
\mho(a)=\{0,{\mathbb{N}}\}
, note that
mk({\mathbb{N}})=1
.

4.

Let
a
be the action of
D({\mathbb{Z}})
on
A({\mathbb{N}})
by multiplication. Then
\mho(a)=\{0,{\mathbb{N}}\}
.

5.

Let
a
be the action of
D({\mathbb{Z}})
on
A({\mathbb{N}})
given by
\lambda\cdot x=\lambda^{2}x
. Then
\mho(a)=\{0,2{\mathbb{N}}\}
, note that
mk(2{\mathbb{N}})=1
.

6.

Let
a
be the action of
D({\mathbb{Z}})
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, then
\mho(a)=\{0,{\mathbb{N}},-{\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{Z}}\}
.

7.

Let
a
be the action of
D({\mathbb{Z}}^{2})
on
A({\mathbb{N}}^{2})
by multiplication, then
\mho(a)=\{0\times 0,{\mathbb{N}}\times 0,0\times{\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{N}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\}
.

8.

Let
a
be the action of
D({\mathbb{Z}}/6{\mathbb{Z}})
on
D({\mathbb{Z}}/6{\mathbb{Z}})\times D({\mathbb{Z}}/6{\mathbb{Z}})\times D({\mathbb{Z}}/6{\mathbb{Z}})
given by

\lambda\cdot(x,y,z)=(\lambda x,\lambda^{2}y,\lambda^{3}z).

Then
\mho(a)=\{{\mathbb{Z}}/6{\mathbb{Z}},2{\mathbb{Z}}/6{\mathbb{Z}},3{\mathbb{Z}}/6{\mathbb{Z}},6{\mathbb{Z}}/6{\mathbb{Z}}\}
.

9.

Let
a
be the action of
D({\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{G}}_{m}
on
A({\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{1\})
where
{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{1\}=[2,3\rangle
is the submonoid of
({\mathbb{N}},+)
generated by
2
and
3
, then
\mho(a)=\{0,{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{1\},2{\mathbb{N}},3{\mathbb{N}}\}
. This shows that Algebraic Magnetism refines strictly some aspects of the theory of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-attractors.

10.

Let
a
be the adjoint action of a maximal split torus
T=D(M)
on a given reductive group scheme
G
. Then
N\mapsto N\cap\Phi
provides a bijection between
\mho(a)
and the set of subsets of
\Phi(G,T)
that are closed under addition. Pure magnets of rank
1
correspond to roots (cf. §
16.5
for more details about the example of reductive groups).

16
Complements, applications and examples

16.1
Tangent spaces and attractors

Let
S
be a scheme. We denote by
I_{S}
the scheme of dual numbers over
S
as in
[
SGA3
, Exp. II Définition 2.1]
. For any scheme
T
over
S
, we have
I_{T}=I_{S}\times_{S}T
.
Explicitly,
I_{S}={\operatorname{Spec}}(\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon])\times_{\mathbb{Z}}S
where
{\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]\overset{\varepsilon=[T]}{=}\mathbb{Z}[T]/(T^{2})}
. Let
X
be a functor over
S
. Let
T_{X}
be the tangent space of
X
as in
[
SGA3
, Exp. II Définition 3.1]
. This is a functor from
{\operatorname{Sch}}_{S}
to
Set
sending a scheme
R
over
S
to

{\operatorname{Hom}}_{R}(I_{R},X_{R}).

Remark 16.1
.

1.

For
S
-morphisms
X\to Y
and
Z\to Y
, we have
T_{X\times_{Y}Z}\cong T_{X}\times_{T_{Y}}T_{Z}
.

2.

If
X\to Y
is a monomorphism, then
T_{X}\to T_{Y}
is a monomorphism.

3.

We have a canonical identification
T_{S}=S
.

Let
M
be an abelian group and assume that
D(M)_{S}
acts on
X
. Then
D(M)_{S}
acts naturally on
T_{X}
using the definition of
T_{X}
. Let
N
be a submonoid in
M
.

Proposition 16.2

We have a canonical isomorphism

(T_{X})^{N}\cong T_{X^{N}}.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
(T_{X})^{N}(S^{\prime})\cong T_{X^{N}}(S^{\prime})
for any
S
-scheme
S^{\prime}
.
We have

\displaystyle(T_{X})^{N}(S^{\prime})

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Hom}}_{S^{\prime}}^{D(M)_{S^{\prime}}}(A(N)_{S^{\prime}},T_{X_{S^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Hom}}_{S^{\prime}}^{D(M)_{S^{\prime}}}(A(N)_{S^{\prime}}\times_{S^{\prime}}I_{S^{\prime}},X_{S^{\prime}})\text{ where }D(M)_{S^{\prime}}\text{ acts trivially on }I_{S^{\prime}}

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Hom}}_{I_{S^{\prime}}}^{D(M)_{I_{S^{\prime}}}}(A(N)_{I_{S^{\prime}}},X_{I_{S^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Hom}}_{S^{\prime}}(I_{S^{\prime}},{X_{S^{\prime}}^{N}})

\displaystyle=(T_{X^{N}})(S^{\prime}).

This shows that we have a canonical isomorphism.
∎

16.2
Lie algebras and attractors

Let
G/S
be a group functor over a scheme
S
. Recall that in this case
T_{G}
is a group functor over
S
and we have two canonical morphisms of group functors
G\to T_{G}
and
T_{G}\to G
by
[
SGA3
, Exp. II]
. Let
e_{S}
be the trivial group over
S
, as
S
-scheme we have
e_{S}=S
. The Lie algebra of
G
is defined as the fiber product

\textstyle{{\operatorname{Lie}}(G)=e_{S}\times_{G}T_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{e_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G}

where
e_{S}\xrightarrow{}G
is the canonical morphism of group functors from
e_{S}
to
G
. As in the previous section, let
M
be an abelian group and assume that
D(M)_{S}
acts on
G
. We assume moreover that this action is compatible with the group structure on
G
, i.e
D(M)_{S}
acts by automorphisms on
G
. Then the induced action of
D(M)_{S}
on
T_{G}
is by group automorphisms. We thus obtain an action of
D(M)_{S}
on
{\operatorname{Lie}}(G)
by group automorphisms.

Remark 16.3
.

1.

For
S
-group functors
G,K,H
with morphisms
G\to K,H\to K
, we have a canonical isomorphism
{\operatorname{Lie}}(G\times_{K}H)\cong{\operatorname{Lie}}(G)\times_{{\operatorname{Lie}}(K)}{\operatorname{Lie}}(H)
.

2.

If
G\to H
is a monomorphism, then
{\operatorname{Lie}}(G)\to{\operatorname{Lie}}(H)
is a monomorphism.

3.

We have a canonical identification
{\operatorname{Lie}}({e_{S}})=e_{S}
.

Proposition 16.4

We have a canonical isomorphism of group functors over
S

\big({\operatorname{Lie}}(G)\big)^{N}\cong{\operatorname{Lie}}(G^{N})

Proof.

Using Proposition
3.7
and Proposition
16.2
, we have

\big({\operatorname{Lie}}(G)\big)^{N}=\big(S\times_{G}T_{G}\big)^{N}\cong S^{N}\times_{G^{N}}{T_{G}}^{N}\cong S\times_{G^{N}}T_{G^{N}}={\operatorname{Lie}}(G^{N}).

∎

Let us fix now a group functor
H
over
S
and a monomorphism
H\to G^{N^{*}}
preserving the group structures. Recall that
G^{N}_{H}
is the attractor with prescribed limit
H
as in Definition
4.1
.

Proposition 16.5

We have a canonical isomorphism of group functors over
S

\big({\operatorname{Lie}}(G)\big)^{N}_{{\operatorname{Lie}}(H)}\cong{\operatorname{Lie}}(G_{H}^{N}).

Proof.

Using Proposition
16.4
and Remark
16.3
we have

\displaystyle{\operatorname{Lie}}(G)^{N}_{{\operatorname{Lie}}(H)}

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Lie}}(G)^{N}\times_{{\operatorname{Lie}}(G)^{N^{*}}}{\operatorname{Lie}}(H)

\displaystyle\cong{\operatorname{Lie}}(G^{N})\times_{{\operatorname{Lie}}(G^{N^{*}})}{\operatorname{Lie}}(H)

\displaystyle\cong{\operatorname{Lie}}(G^{N}\times_{G^{N^{*}}}H)

\displaystyle={\operatorname{Lie}}(G_{H}^{N})

This shows that we have the desired canonical isomorphism.
∎

16.3
Semidirect products

Let
G/S
be an algebraic group in the sense of
[
Mi17
]
. Here
S={\operatorname{Spec}}(k)
is a field. Let
M
be a finite type abelian group and let
D(M)_{S}
be the associated diagonalizable group scheme. Assume that
D(M)_{S}
acts on
G
by group automorphisms. Let
N\subset M
be a submonoid. Recall that
G^{N}
and
G^{N^{*}}
are sub-algebraic-groups of
G
over
S
(e.g. combine
8.3
,
3.18
,
3.17
and
3.14
) and that we have a canonical morphism
G^{N}\to G^{N^{*}}
of algebraic group over
S
(cf.
3.15
). The kernel of this morphism is
G^{N}_{e_{G}}
, the attractor with prescribed limit
e_{G}
(cf. §
4
).

Proposition 16.6

The attractor
G^{N}
is the semidirect product of
G_{e_{G}}^{N}
and
G^{N^{*}}
. In particular, the multiplication map
G_{e_{G}}^{N}\rtimes G^{N^{*}}\to G^{N}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Note that
G^{N^{*}}
and
G^{N}_{e_{G}}
are sub-algebraic-groups of
G^{N}
. There is a morphism
G^{N}\to G^{N^{*}}
whose restriction to
G^{N^{*}}
is the identity and whose kernel is
G^{N}_{e_{G}}
. Then Proposition
16.6
follows from
[
Mi17
, 2.34]
.
∎

Remark 16.7
.

Proposition
16.6
extends
[
Mi17
, Theorem 13.33 (b)]
.

16.4
Relation to
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-attractors

Let
X
be an
S
-algebraic space with and action of
D(M)_{S}
. Let
N
be a submonoid of the group
M
. Then in some cases the attractor space
X^{N}
can be obtained as a succession of attractors under
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
(cf. the introduction and
[
Ri16
]
) and fixed-points. Let us gives two examples.

1.

Let
\alpha\in M
and let us consider
X^{[\alpha\rangle}
. Under mild assumptions, by Proposition
3.31
we have
X^{[\alpha\rangle}=\big(X^{(\alpha)}\big)^{[\alpha\rangle}
. By Proposition
3.33

X^{(\alpha)}
identifies with the fixed-points space
X^{D(M/(\alpha))_{S}}
. So the operation
X\rightsquigarrow X^{(\alpha)}
can be realized as taking fixed-points. By Remarks
3.12
and
3.11
,
D((\alpha))_{S}
and
D(M)_{S}
act on
X^{(\alpha)}
, and using Proposition
3.30
, we have
\big(X^{(\alpha)}\big)^{[\alpha\rangle\subset(\alpha)}=\big(X^{(\alpha)}\big)^{[\alpha\rangle\subset M}
(cf. Remark
3.4
for the notation
{X}^{N\subset M}
). But
\big([\alpha\rangle\subset(\alpha)\big)\simeq\big(\mathbb{N}\subset\mathbb{Z}\big)
, so the operation
X^{(\alpha)}\rightsquigarrow\big(X^{(\alpha)}\big)^{[\alpha\rangle}
can be realized as taking attractor under
\mathbb{G}_{m}=D(\mathbb{Z})_{S}
. So
X\rightsquigarrow X^{[\alpha\rangle}
can be realized as fixed-points followed by taking the attractor under an action of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
.

2.

Assume that
M=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
and that
N=\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
. Using Propositions
3.31
and
3.30
, we have

X^{N}=\big(X^{{\mathbb{N}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}}\big)^{{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}}=\big(X^{{\mathbb{N}}\times 0\subset{\mathbb{Z}}\times 0}\big)^{0\times{\mathbb{N}}\subset 0\times{\mathbb{Z}}}.

This shows that
X\rightsquigarrow X^{N}
can be realized as two stages of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-attractors.

3.

If
M={\mathbb{Z}}^{r}
and
N={\mathbb{N}}^{r}
then
X\rightsquigarrow X^{N}
can be realized as
r
stages of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-attractors.

16.5
Magnetic point of view on reductive groups

This section is devoted to the observation that parabolic and Levi subgroups of reductive group schemes are easily described using attractors. We work with a split reductive group over a field for simplicity and accessibility but similar results hold more generally (e.g. cf. Proposition
16.16
and
[
ALRR22
, §6.3]
). In fact, we expect attractors theory provide a natural framework to study some aspects of the advanced theory of group schemes from the beginning, but this is not the purpose of the present work.
So let
G
be a split connected reductive group scheme over a field
R
. Let
T
be a maximal split torus and choose a Borel
B
containing
T
.
Let
\Phi=\Phi(G,T)\subset X^{*}(T)
denote the set of roots associated to
(G,T)
and
\Phi^{+}=\Phi(B,T)
the roots in
B
. Let
\mathcal{B}
be the basis of
\Phi
determined by
\Phi^{+}.
For
\alpha\in\Phi
, let
U_{\alpha}
be the associated unipotent root group and
\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}

be the root group associated to
\alpha
in the Lie algebra of
G
. We refer to
[
SGA3
, Exp. XXII]
for the definition of
U_{\alpha}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}
.
Let
U
be the unipotent radical of
B
and let
\mathfrak{u}\subset\mathfrak{b}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be the Lie algebras of
U,B
and
G
.
Consider the adjoint action of
T
on
G,U
and
\mathfrak{u}
.

Proposition 16.8

There exists a
T
-equivariant isomorphism of
R
-schemes
\mathfrak{u}\simeq U
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
[
SGA3
, Exp. XXII Th. 1.1, Exp. XXVI Prop. 1.12]
, indeed these results imply the following assertions. For each root
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
, we have a
T
-equivariant isomorphism
U_{\alpha}\simeq\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}
.
We have
T
-equivariant isomorphisms of schemes
\mathfrak{u}=\Pi_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}
and
U=\Pi_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}U_{\alpha}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Let us now fix
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
. Recall that
[\alpha\rangle\subset X^{*}(T)
is the submonoid generated by
\alpha
.

Proposition 16.9

We have a canonical isomorphism
\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}\simeq\mathfrak{u}^{[\alpha\rangle}
(resp.
U_{\alpha}\simeq U^{[\alpha\rangle}
), between root group and
[\alpha\rangle
-attractor for the action of
T={\operatorname{Spec}}(R[X^{*}(T)])
on
\mathfrak{u}
(resp.
U
).

Proof.

By Proposition
16.8
, we have a
T
-equivariant isomorphism
\mathfrak{u}\simeq U
, so it is enough to prove the statement for
\mathfrak{u}
. Since
G
is split,
\Phi
is reduced and Proposition
3.27
finishes the proof.
∎

Recall that we have a bijection between parabolic subgroups of
G
containing
B
and subsets of
\mathcal{B}
, cf e.g.
[
Co14
, Page 35, lines 4-5]
or
[
CGP10
, 2.2.8]
.
If
\Sigma
is a subset of
X^{*}(T)
, we also use the notation
N_{\Sigma}
to denote
[\Sigma\rangle
, the monoid generated by
\Sigma
in
X^{*}(T)
.

Proposition 16.10

Let
\zeta\subset\mathcal{B}
. Let
\Theta
be
\zeta\cup-\zeta
. Let
\Sigma
be
\mathcal{B}\cup-\zeta
.

1.

The attractor
G^{N_{\Theta}}
is the Levi subgroup
L_{\Theta}
such that
\Phi(L_{\Theta},T)=N_{\Theta}\cap\Phi
.

2.

The attractor
G^{N_{\Sigma}}
is the associated parabolic subgroup, moreover
L_{\Theta}
is a Levi component of
P
.

3.

Let
\xi\subset\zeta
. Let
\Gamma=\mathcal{B}\cup-\xi
. Let
N
be a submonoid of
N_{\Sigma}
such that
N\cap\Sigma=\Gamma
. Then
G^{N}=G^{N_{\Gamma}}
.

Proof.

Let
P_{\Sigma}
be the parabolic corresponding to
\zeta
.
By
[
CGP10
, 2.2.8, 2.2.9]
, there exists a
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)
such that
P_{\Sigma}
is the attractor associated to the monoid
{\mathbb{N}}

relatively to the action of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}=D({\mathbb{Z}})_{S}
on
G
via
x.g={\operatorname{ad}}_{\lambda(x)}g
and such that
\lambda(\beta)\geqslant 0
for all
\beta\in\Sigma
and
\lambda(\beta)=0
for all
\beta\in\Theta
.
The Levi subgroup
L_{\Theta}
corresponding to
\Theta
is the fixed space in
G
under the action of
\lambda
by conjuguation, i.e
L_{\Theta}=G^{0}
.
Now we prove the Proposition.

1.

Assume first that
\zeta=\mathcal{B}
. Then
L_{\Theta}=G
and
N_{\Theta}=N_{\Phi(G,T)}
.
Using Prop.
16.9
, we deduce that the big cell

\Omega=\Pi_{\alpha\in\Phi^{-}}U_{\alpha}\times T\times\Pi_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}U_{\alpha}
is in
G^{N_{\Theta}}
. Now we have inclusions
\Omega\subset G^{N_{\Theta}}\subset G
with
\Omega
dense in
G
and
G^{N_{\Theta}}
closed in
G
. This implies
G^{N_{\Theta}}=G
.
Let us now prove the general case, let
\zeta\subset\mathcal{B}
.
By Proposition
3.30
, we have
G^{f^{-1}(0)}=G^{0}=L_{\Theta}
. We have
\Theta\subset f^{-1}(0)
, and so
N_{\Theta}\subset f^{-1}(0)
,
consequently
G^{N_{\Theta}}\subset G^{f^{-1}(0)}.
So we have proved that
G^{N_{\Theta}}\subset L
and let us now prove that this is an equality. We remark that
N_{\Theta}=N_{\Phi(L_{\Theta},T)}
.
Now since
L\subset G
and using the first case done before, we have
L=L^{N_{\Phi(L_{\Theta},T)}}\subset G^{N}_{\Theta}
. This finishes the proof.

2.

Recall that
\lambda:{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\to T
corresponds to the morphism of abelian groups
f:X^{*}(T)\to{\mathbb{Z}},\chi\mapsto(\lambda,\chi)
.
Now we see
G
as a
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-scheme and as a
T
-scheme. By Proposition
3.30
, we have
G^{{\mathbb{N}}}=G^{f^{-1}({\mathbb{N}})}
.
Since, for all
\beta\in\Sigma
,
f(\beta)=\lambda(\beta)\geqslant 0
, we have
f(\beta)\in{\mathbb{N}}
and so
\Sigma\subset f^{-1}({\mathbb{N}})
, and so
N_{\Sigma}\subset f^{-1}({\mathbb{N}})
.
Consequently,
P_{\Sigma}=G^{{\mathbb{N}}}=G^{f^{-1}({\mathbb{N}})}\supset G^{N_{\Sigma}}
. Let us prove that

P_{\Sigma}\subset G^{N_{\Sigma}}
.
We have
P_{\Sigma}=L_{\Theta}\times\mathrm{R_{u}}(P_{\Sigma})
where
\mathrm{R_{u}}(P_{\Sigma})
is the unipotent radical of
P_{\Sigma}
.
Using (i), we have
L_{\Theta}=L_{\Theta}^{N_{\Theta}}\subset L_{\Theta}^{N_{\Sigma}}\subset L_{\Theta}
, and so
L_{\Theta}^{N_{\Sigma}}=L_{\Theta}
. Using Proposition
16.9
, one has
(\mathrm{R_{u}}(P_{\Sigma}))^{N_{\Sigma}}=\mathrm{R_{u}}(P_{\Sigma})
.
So
P_{\Sigma}^{N_{\Sigma}}=P_{\Sigma}
, and so
P_{\Sigma}\subset G^{N_{\Sigma}}
.

3.

We have a canonical closed immersion
G^{N_{\Gamma}}\subset G^{N}
. We have
N\cap N_{\Theta}=N_{\zeta\cup-\xi}
and
N\cap N_{\Theta}
it is thus included in
N_{\Gamma}
. By Proposition
3.23
we have
G^{N}=(G^{N_{\Sigma}})^{N}
.
By the previous assertions we have
G^{N_{\Sigma}}=G^{N_{\Theta}}\times\mathrm{R_{u}}(G^{N_{\Sigma}})
. Now by
3.23
we have

\Big(G^{N_{\Theta}}\times\mathrm{R_{u}}(G^{N_{\Sigma}})\Big)^{N}=(G^{N_{\Theta}})^{N}\times(\mathrm{R_{u}}(G^{N_{\Sigma}}))^{N}=G^{N_{\zeta\cup-\xi}}\times\mathrm{R_{u}}(G^{N_{\Sigma}}).

This implies that
G^{N_{\Gamma}}\supset G^{N}
and finishes the proof.

∎

Remark 16.11
.

Proposition
16.10
implies that any parabolic or Levi subgroup of
G
containing
T
can be obtained as an attractor under the conjuguation action of
T
on
G
. Moreover, assume that
\mathbf{B}
is a parabolic subgroup in a parabolic
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{L}
is a Levi component of
\mathbf{P}
. We assume that
\mathbf{B},\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{L}
contain
T
. Let
\mathbf{M}
be
\mathbf{B}\cap\mathbf{L}
, this is a parabolic subgroup in
L
. Then one has a cartesian square
